WOMAN'S REALM.

DRESS-FASHION-HOME.

OUTDOOR GARMENTS.

If there is one thing more than another in which we have developed originality, says the Queen, it is in the shape of our current outdoor garments. They are very freakish. We use curious materials and quaint embroideries, taking our inspiration from the Orient, Russia, and the Norse. Some of the cabuchons are truly barbaric. The only thing that characterises all such garments would seem to be the 'absence of a high collar. They most of them fall closely to the shoulders. and then descend in ends or open widely or hang in rich folds in front. Beautiful linings of vivid rose colour and rich brocade tharacterise many of the new models. Some are shawl shaped, and others have sling sleeves and angel sleeves, as well as cape-like sleeves. Many of the embroideries are of the Persian order, and now we are wearing a great deal of velvet.

===== 0002601_19080111_art0010.txt | included=true | included =====
A SMART COSTUME.

T

1

Very smart is the chic little costume pictured in our sketch, which has just come over from Paris, where costumes of a similar type are creating quite a furore just now. The upper part of the costume consists of a plain, close, semi-fitting coat of the darkost green corduroy

. is bound all round its edges a strapping of plain dark-green silk braid. This coat can be worn either open or closed, but when open it displays a mannish little waistcoat of biscuit cloth, fastened down the middle with a row of 'outtons. The skirt which accompanies this coat is of the pleated type, and is carried out in tartan' of a very sombre tone, almost if not quite identical with the Black Watch plaid with which we are all so familiar. Thi...4 costume. I need hardly say, is intended strictly for country wear, the corduroy standing any amount of wear and tear and bad weather with impunity. Worn with a simple hat the effect is exceedingly smart, and the eostumo is undeniably becoming. A SMART VELVET FROCK.

Striped velveteens are in great demand, both in Paris and London. It has been discovered by acme of the more intelligent of our sex, says a Globe writer, that a striped velveteen does not shew marks of chair or sofa so much as un- striped. The same is true of velvet and velvet chiffon. There is a pinkish tone of grey which is seen upon the necks of some doves, and also in a velveteen gown made for a beautiful prin. eess. The mode is as follows: The skirt is abso. lutely tight below the waist, hut an immense amount of fulness is let in in pointed gores. The beginning of these is hidden by a bias band of , the velveteen which goes entirely round tho skirt. The bodice is cut away in a small curve round the back of the neck, and in two deeper curves in front, a bias band of velveteen inter- vening between the two, and laid upon a very full blouse of deep cream-coloured mimosa lace. the design of which is outlined with pale gold thread. The collar of this blouse is carried very high, and, in fact, ascends in points behind the ears, conveying almost an idea of strangulation. The sleeves are full as far as the elbow, and thence are composed of the lace with several rows of velveteen, each outlined with pale gold chain stitch, -

PREMIUM ON BABIES.

Mayor Offers £1 Prize to 100 Parents.

sG.;:a"

At the meeting of the Halifax Town Council on Wednesday night the Nlayor,Alderman William Wallace, announced that he would offer .0 t to the parents of each of the first hundred children born in the borough this year,in all cases in which the children reached one year of age. The conditions, said his Worship,were as follows The children must be born on or after January Ist, 1908. The birth of each child wtist he reported to the medical officer within 48 hours of its birth. The medical officer will keep the register of the children and the parents of the first 100 reported, 'alto will be eligible to receive the on the child attain- ing the age of twelve months.

7.t8 soon as practicable after the birth is repr.rted, the parents will b., given a ptornissory note to tile va,itie of £l, payable only after the child attains the ape of one year. The announcement N% as received with applause.

The Examiner, Saturday January 11, 1908.

[ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.]

THE TURN OF THE WHEEL.

BY DOROTHY BAIRD, &uthor of "My Beautiful Boy," "The Imper- Calcine of Mr. Scroggins," "The Story of a Look," "The Girl with the Sweet Face," &c.

The short December afternoon was swiftly drawing to its close. Heavy grey clouds, racing before a cruel wind, had long ago hidden the wan isun from sight, and a premature darkness had spread over the great city. Presently rain began to fall, quickly changing into sleet—odd and miserable as only sleet can be. And all the while the wind raged mercilessly, whistling down the narrow alleys, roaring in the wide thoroughfares, beating round sharp corners, and drivin, the sleet before it with stinging force. The wretched afternoon was turning into a wild night. Reginald Messon plodded wearily northwards, the collar of his threadbare coat turned up, and his head well down to meet the gale. He was still quite young, in spite of his grey hair and wrinkled race; the lines were those of trouble, not of age. And he was a gentleman in spite of his shabby clothes and worn-out boots—but a gentleman in the direst straits, and, to judge by the expression of his face, one in the very depths of despair. Only a short time ago he had been in posses- sion of a moderate income—an income which had enabled him to marry without a shadow d) f anxiety for the future, and which left him free Lo follow his literary pursuits for the pure love of them, and not for the necessity of earning money. For Reginald Masson was a thinker— not an irresponsible dreamer, but one who studied human life with the desire of doing what he could to make it nobler and better. He wrote very little, and spent the rest of his time in thinking, observing, reading—storing his mind with knowledge that must be of use to him sooner or later. There was no necessity for him te hurry with his work, and so he laboured on at his leisure' putting his whole soul into the book, and Doris helped him.

Doris was not in the least clever. She was a homely, domesticated little soul, never happier than when she was doing odd jobs about the house, or darning her husband's socks. She often said that she was an absurd wife for so intellec- tual a man. And yet, perhaps, the book would never have been written but for her. It was she who took his notes and copied them legibly, putting them away in such order that she could always lay her hand on the particular fact he wanted. She wrote for hours at his dictation, and always transcribed the MSS. clearly after he had corrected them. More than that, her heart was as much in the work as his was, and when he was inclined to be downhearted or discouraged at the magnitude of the task he had set before him, it was she who coaxed away his misgivings and set him to work afresh, with higher aspira- tions and renewed courage. The book was nearing completion when the crash came. Reginald's mind was so full of the sorrows of the world, its sin and misery, its fleeting joys and changing hopes, that it never entered his head to watch the way his own affairs were trending. When the bank in which all his money was invested stopped payment, he was totally unprepared for the blew. After every- thing was settled he found himself practically penniless, and with only the book to rely on for provision for the future. Still, he did not despair. The book was good; modest as he was he was convinced of that, and he was a man of stern judgment. They both had wild dreams as to the profits which should accrue from it, and for a time they worked on cheer- fully, trying to forget how near the wolf was drawing to their door. Then Doris began to droop, tired out with stress of work and fruitless economising. The baby came into the world all too soon, and after wailing a feeble protest at the hardness of its lot had fallen asleep to wake no more. In spite of her heavy sorrow, the young mother never allowed her cheerfulness to !fag as the weeks went by, and their little store of money dwindled. They had to sell up their -home at last and move into cheap lodgings. Here Reginald finished his book and sent it out, and both he and Doris tried for daily situations. But situations are hard to find, especially when the face of the applicant is worn with anxiety and his clothes are shabby. Presently the book came back with a polite note of refusal. It was good, the publisher said, but it would never be popular. He did not feel justified in making any offer for it, but if Mr. Masson cared to pay the cost of publication he would undertake it, &c.

This happened, not once, but many times. Nobody would take the book, nobody would employ the author. Doris failed to obtain a situation. Now they were reduced to a garret in a very poor quarter, and starvation stared them in the face.

1— He had started out that day with renewed hope. One of the leading magazines had accepted a short story and he was going to ask for immediate payment. Also he had been recom- mended to a post as private secretary to a successful novelist, and he had little doubt but that he would obtain it. All the way to the City he had amused himself by looking in at the shops and planning what he would take home to Doris—poor, cheerful Doris in her fireless attic, with nothing in the cupboard for dinner. He had positively gloated in the thought of seeing her eat a real good meal once more, and warm her numbed limbs before a blazing tire. But now the last hope was gone. The magazine only paid its contributors on the publication of their work, and would in no case make an exception to this rule; the secretaryship had been given to someone with more (xperimice. Reginald plodded homewards with bitter feelings in his heart. He would give up the writing that was so dear to him, and set to work on light literature, the frothy nothings that are in such demand at the leading libraries, the blood- curdling stories of crime and criminals which pander to the popular taste for highly-coloured literature. There was money in such stuff; there was none in lofty ideals and good writing. He had striven after the best and it had brought him low; he would aim lower, it might raise him high in this world's goods. The shops which had called up such happy day-dreams in the morning only added to his anguish now. Once ho stopped and gazed with hungry, wolfish eyes at some food in a cook-shop, but he quickly turned away with a choking sob. Not for himself; he forgot his own hunger entirely in the thought that Doris must go supperless to bed. Anxious as he was to get back to her, his steps slackened almost unconsciously as ho neared their humble dwelling. It was so hard to have to go back to her like this. Not that she would grumble—she never did that even in their darkest days—only her bright eyes would cloud for a moment, and she would bite her lips to keep them from trembling while she gained enough control over her voice to speak cheerfully and comfortingly. Her very courage made it harder to disappoint hor, for he knew what it cost her to be brave.

She was waiting on the landing as he came up the stairs.

"Is that you P" she called, for the staircase was winding and ill-lighted. "Yes," he answered, and he knew that the very sound of his voice must tall her that his miNsion had been fruitless.

"Oh, you poor, wet hey!" 3Le exclaimed as she embraced him. "Tale off your coat at once and hang it on the bannisters to dry. And then, oh! Reg, I have such a surprise for you." Her tone was more than usually cheerful. He caused, to hold her in his arms a moment, and laid his cheek to hers. • It was good to be together, no matter what misery was ahead. She returned his caress with a hearty hug, end then seized him by the arm and led him Co the attic door.

--• "Oh! I am so excited," she said. "See!" She flung open the door, and stood triumphant. A cheerful fire burnt in the little grate, a tin of cocoa flanked the cups and saucers on the table, err!. the whole r_oom Wfl.• redolent of the ersteful

Odoftr or hot sausages. Reginald could not speak. The change from the gloomy scene he had pictured all the way home was too great, and his eyes filled with tears. Doris understood. _

"I did it all myself," she said, dragging him to a chair before the fire. "Pull off your boots. Look, I have warmed your slippers, and here is a pair of dry socks. I think darned socks are warmer than now ones, don't you?" And she held up a pair that certainly needed some recom- menation, for they showed more darning than •original texture. Her manner was irresistible. Reeinald smiled up at her, though the tears stood in nul eyes. "How—" he began.

But she stopped him with a kiss. "I shan't tell you anything till we've had supper," she said. "Do be quick with thoso toots. I'm so hungry. The kettle's just on the boil, and there's heaps of milk and sugar, and a lovely brown loaf, and—guess what besides." . . . .

"Sausages," ventured -Reginald, beginning to laugh. And mashed potatoes," she returned, dancing backwards and forwards from table to fireplace. "Come along, it's all ready, and oh, won't we just stuff.," Was there ever such a happy meal? Reginald thought it impossible. Certainly no food had ever tasted so delicious as the homely sausages and mashed potatoes, no nectar could form so generous a beverage as the rich brown cocoa, poured out by Doris. Doris was like a presiding genius of joy, she was so happy, and her pretty, pathetic little face so radiant. All through the meal she kept up a light flow of merry chatter, and she would answer no questions till the table was cleared. Then she drew up a stool close to Rec,inald's chair, and, producing a tiny screw of tobacco, proceeded to fill her husband's pipe, which had, alas! lain empty for so long. "It's only just a whiff for to-night," she said. "But you shall have more to-morrow." And she laughed gleefully at the sight of his pleased face, and ran to open the door for the poor little kitten who lived a miserable, hunted life in the base- ment, and who came to her sometimes for comfort. And she was so happy that she gave it all the milk which had been saved for their morning meal. "We shan't miss it," she said. Reginald waited till she had subsided upon her stool again, then he placed his hand under her chin and turned her face to his. "What does it all mean ? " he asked. "It means that I have found work," she answered, gaily. "Work that will last for some little time any way." _

, .. . "Tell me about it," he said, puffin.,' luxuriously at his pipe. The witch had got hold of his favourite tobacco.

"I was so miserable after you had gone to-day, Reg," replied she, nestling up closer to him. "It was so cold, and I was so hungry, and I knew you were cold and hungry too. And I knew also that you wouldn't be successful. I just sat hero feeling about as low as I could feel, and presently my thoughts went back to the dear old days, and I felt that I must go and look at the, house—our first home, dear—and so I tramped all the way out to Hampstead. It's all just thc same as it used to be, only they've put up a summer-house where our old arbour used to stand, and the curtains at the windows aren't half so pretty as mine were." She paused. It was hard to speak casually of the place which had been so dear to her.

"I walked up and down the lane for such a long time, Reg, she went on, bravely. "Somehow I couldn't summon up courage to leave it, and there was nothing but hopelessness to come back to. And as I walked backwards and forwards I tried to think what I could do to earn money. You know I'm not clever, I don't think anyone will ever take me as a governess; the very questions they ask me frighten me out of my life. Of course, I would like to go and read to some old lady or gentleman every morning, and write letters, and so on; but so would a great many others, and really there aren't enough old people to go round. I couldn't think of anything else but charing, and I didn't feel quite up to that! I was just giving it up in despair, and was taking my last look at the old place, when the door opened and two ladies came down the path._

4 " 'I don't know what to do,' said one, who was evidently the lady of the house. The children are invited to so many parties, and I'm all in arrears with their work. I'd give anything to hear of a good sewing woman who would come to the house by the day.' " 'l'm sorry I don't know of anyone,' replica her friend. I was trying for the same thin,' last week. I could only get an ex-lady's-maid, who was too proud to do this and that and the other,_ and grumbled at the food.'

" Sewing women seem the rarest thing in the world,' said the first lady. 'I am sure I could employ anyone for a,fortnight, for there are lots of house things that want doing, and I would pay well. I shouldn't mind that.' "They shook hands and parted. Reg dear, I could have screamed with joy, for, you know, I can sow very nicely' when I try. I could hardly keep from dancing !ip the path, but by a great effort I managed to walk sedately. I told Mrs. Cameron—that was the lady's name—that I had overheard some of her conversation, and I offered my services, which were accepted on the spot." Reginald smoothed her hair very tenderly. "My little Doris a sewing woman! " he murmured, and relief and pain struggled for mastery in his voice. "Has it come to that ? "

_ "Hush!" she said. "It was very nice. I enjoyed it. All day long I have been sitting in the dear old study, and there was such a cosy lire, Reg, and :inch good things to cat. Oh dear me! I felt so horribly greedy when I thought of my poor old man dinnerless out in the cold. But there's heaps of work to be done. You needn't ,worry any more. You can sit at home and write some short stories, and by tie time my work gives out the money for yours will be coining in. I don't think my work will give out for a very long time," she added, sagely. "Mrs. Cameron says she knows plenty of People who will only be too thankful to have me.". "Doris, somehow I don't like the idea," he said. "It seems all wrong and topsy-turvey that you should be earning the bread, while I- oh! my dearest, everything I touch seems to be a failure." In an instant her arms were round his neck. "You don't know yet," she said. "Only six publishers have seen your book. It does not follow that because they have refused it everyone else will do so. Don't you remember old Mr. Roland saying that you had a great future before you if only you would be patient? He is very wise, yau know—much wiser than your little wife.',

He made a.gesture of impatience. "Oh, yea, I knows" he said. "But I can't make money—l can't keep my wife. Instead, she is keeping me. I have been thinking about it all the way home, and I have made up my mind to give up serious writing and scribble love stories and sensational detective stories; I might even write a melodrama for provincial touring companies. There is money in all that." And he laughed—a hitter laugh that made Doris's heart ache. "You will do nothing of the kind," she answered, firmly. "That would be tiikiw, your one talent and using it for ignoble ends, which is worse than wrappin7 it up in a napkin and bury- ing it, for that at llast does no harm to others. You can do better, and you ought to do so. No, no; you will be bravo! You will never write a line that you could be ashamed of, or that I could be ashamed of for you. You will be a power in the world, and a power for good." Ile was silent, and she continued, caressingly: "You art' going to write a lot of short stories for they will help to keep the pot boiling till the book is taken—nice stories, you know, like the book. And you might do some essays on the queer side of life you have been seeing lately. And every day you must, work a little at the new book. You haven't even begun it, and you told me the plot ages ago. I shall look forward ta coming home eften night and hearing all you have written." •

ke saiu, drawing a slip of his pocket and handing it to her. It was a cheque for five pounds. Doris's eyes danced. "What is it for ?" she cried, when utterance was vouchsafed her. "It's for a story I wrote a few days ago—the day after you first went to Mrs. Cameron. 1 haven't shewn it to you—" "Oh, Reg, to write a story and not show it to me!" The tones were very reproachful. "No, dear," lie said, penitently. "I didn't want you to see it. You'd have made me alter it if you had." Doris's eyes grew wide with fear. "Is there something in it I shouldn't like?" she asked. His face broke into a merry smile. "No, child, it isn't that at all," he answered. "But you are the model for the heroine, and I was afraid you'd find it out and want me to put in a lot of faults which wouldn't be true to the original--" Doris stopped his lips with her little cold hand. "You are exceedingly ridiculous," she said, as they turned to walk on. "But I suppose since you have earned five pounds I must forgive you." "There's another letter at home," he said, hesitatingly. "It's from Mordaunt about the book. But I didn't open it, dear, I hadn't the courage without you. A disappointment is so much worse if you aren't by." "To share it, eh? " laughed Doris. "Well, if it is a disappointment it won't matter much now, will it ? Let's forget all about it for a time and enjoy our shopping." It was very late when they climbed the stairs to their humble abode. Doris pronounced it to have been the most successful shopping expedi- tion she had ever undertaken; they had even managed to purchase a gorgeous silk tie as a present for the landlady, who had been good to them in their troubles, and they had ordered an extra supply of milk for the kitten to be left each morning. Moreover, the five pounds was untouched. Reginald was very silent during their walit home, and as soon as they had lighted the candle he handed her an envelope. "Read it," he said. "1 daren't." He did not dare to watch her face as stu mastered its contents; and by the time she had finished the perusal she had no voice left, so that she was fain to hold the sheet before his eyes. After all he was bound to read it for himself. They did not speak for a long time after that, only clung to each other very closely, and Doris cried a little. After a bit she stroked hif shabby coat lovingly. "No more thin clothes for my sweetheart," she said, with a little choke in her voice. "Nor for mine," he responded. She never seemed to remember that her dress had been originally intended for summer wear. In a moment she looked up, half laughter and half tears. "I suppose we shall have to leave this old garret ? " she said. "I believe you're sorry, Doris." She nodded. "We've been so happy here,' she whispered. "And so hungry and cold," he replied. "But we loved each other, and so it didn't matter. And now we wouldn't have it other- wise, would we?" Reginald shook his head. "It would have been very different but for you," he said. And so new and better days dawned fox Reginald and Doris—days which made their troubles seem like the memory of a bad dream. But the memory is not altogether unhappy, fox they emerged from that bitter time with a deeper love for one another, a fuller understanding of the blessed truth that "God's in His Heaven. CORPORATION EMPLOYEES AS AUXILIARY POSTMEN. Councillor STOTT asked the Chairman of the Gas Committee :- 1. What number of men from the Gas Department were acting as auxiliary postmen during the Christmas and New Year weeks ? 2, On what days were they employed ? 3. Did any of them receive wages from the Corpora- tion for any portion of the time they were engaged on Post Office work ? Alderman Pierpoint's answers were : I. Ten meter inspectors. 2. December 23rd, 24th, and 25th. 3. No, except on Christmas Day. This was a holiday for which they were paid, and they worked for the Post Office from early morning until 11 a.m. Similar questions were put to the Chairman of the Electricity and Tramways Committee with regard to his department. The MAYOR replied :- 1, One. 2. Christmas Day. 3. No. Councillor STEEL said it had been mentioned that Councillor Stott had given his reasons for asking the questions to the chairman et the committee. Could those reasons not be made pdblic ? Councillor STOTT said that strong representations were made to him that preference had been given by the Postmaster. who had it perfect right to select whom he liked, to Corporation servants, when there were men of good character on the register at the municipal labour bureau. They felt that unemployed men who could be relied upon might have been engaged in prefer- ence to men who were receiving a weekly wage. It was also said that the Corporation servants were being paid their wages during the time they were doing post office work. As the matter was mentioned to him (Councillor Stott) in public, he thought the questions ought to be answered publicly. ' The MAYOR : You see your information is entirely Incorrect. You should not take notice of people who ' tell such tales. (Laughter.) COMPLAINT ABOUT VIE GAS. Upon the minutes of the Gas Committee, Councillor HAWTIIORN asked why was it the town was supplied with such poor gas at Christmas time. The pressure was not only low but fluctuating and it had been impossible to remain indocrs with comfort. lie would be glad if the mattercould receive attention. Alderman PIERPOiNT said it bad been certified that the average illuminating power of the gas had been equal to 18,1 candles, and that was above the average. As to the fluctuation, that must have been due to the fittings not being kept properly clean. Any complaints as to fittings would receive attention at the gas office.

- - - SHOP DOOR THEFTS.

Bad Use of the New Order.

At the Borough Court on Monday, hefore the Mayor, and other magistrates, Edwin Mullins. Dial Court, was charged with stealing,on Saturday January 4th,frorn the sbop of Charles Price, two pieces of bacon and further with stealing from outside the shop of Thomas Lee, draper, a roll of cloth value 10s. Prisoner pleaded guilty to the first but not to the second charge. The Chief Constable said that on Satnrday evening the prisoner was seen by Mr. Cunliffe in Buttermarket- street, to take a piece of bacon from the shop named. The police were informed and prisoner on being arrested at once admitted the theft. The police went to the house of prisoner's mother and discovered the stolen bacon in the cupboard. Prisoner was also seen outside Messrs. Lee's shop in Buttermarket-street by the manager, Mr. Eaton, and later the cloth was missed. Prisoner's mother was again visited and the cloth found in her house. On the way to the police station prisoner denied the theft but when charged admitted it. Herbert Eaton said he last saw the cloth tied up outside the shop at 430 on Saturday. The cloth had not been sold. He went to prisoner mother's house and told her that is if she let him have the cloth back it wonid he-alright hut the polka had been informed. Mary Messenger,sister of the prisonv,said her brother brought the rc.ll of cloth home and went out. She went and told mother about it as she thought he had not got it honestly. The Chief Constable said rrisoner only came out of gaol on Saturday hut he would have remained in until the 18th bad it. not been for the new order and he (the Chief Constable) would he glad if prisoner was given the full turn. .

The Mayor said the prisoner had made very bad use of the orrtPr and the bench meant to mark their dis- approval of his conduct. He would have to go to gaol for two months on each charge. THEFT OF A SEIRT.

The Examiner, Saturday, January 11, l9OB PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS

To Wash Scarlet Flannel.—Mix a handful of flour in a quart of cold water, and boil ten minutes. To this add some warm suds made with castile soap, and then wash the flannel gently, rinsing rather than rubbing it. Now rinse in twc or three waters of the same temperature—jusi comfortably warm to the hands, not hotter—an hang out to dry in the shade. By this method the colour should remain unchanged in the flan- nel after many washings. For the Complexion.—lt is a very good plac to take a warm bath every night before retiring This cleanses the body. A cool sponge in tht morning invigorates one. Glycerine and rose. water agree very well with most skins, but th glycerine shonld not be too strong. Diet much to do with the complexion. Avoid highlc seasoned foods and rich fancy and desserts; sub- stitute fruits. Drink plenty of water between meals. Mock Terrapin.—Cut bits of cold roasted fowl, turkey, or duck in cubes of one inch. Measure; to each pint allow two tablesnoen- , fuls of butter, one tablespoonful of flour, linif a pint of milk, and the hard-boiled yolks of three eggs. Rub the butter and flour together, add ' . the milk, stir until boiling; add this gradually to the yolks of the eggs, rubbing all the while. When you have a perfectly smooth, thick, • yellow sauce, add the chicken; stand it over hot water for a least twenty minutes, add a level teaspoonful of salt, a saltspoonful of white pepper, and a teaspoonful of suitable sauce. Serve smoking hot:—"London Journal."

Patterdale Puthlings.—The weight of two eggs in flour, sugar, and butter; beat the eggs; then add flour and sugar gradually, the butter melted, a little salt, the rind of half a lemon grated, and a few currants. Bake in well- buttered teacups for twenty-five minutes, and serve with wine sauce.

Helena Pudding.—Four oz. stale bread- crumbs, 2oz. sugar, loz. butter, loz. candi?cl peel, 2 eggs, some raspberry (or other) jam, and 1 pint of new milk. Boil the milk and pour it over the breadcrumhs, and allow them to soak, fifteen minutes ; then add the beaten yolks of eggs, butter, sugar, and peel chopped fine. Pour this into a buttered piedish, ecread the jam on the top, then add the whites of the eggs whisked to a, stiff froth. Bake for half an hour, or until a light golden brown.

Florence Pudding.—Take }lb. each of flour, ground rice, brown sugar, suet (chopped fine) and currants; teaspoonful of carbonate of soda and a pinch, of salt. Mix all these ingredients well together with a teacupful of milk. Boil 31- hours. The addition of one or two eggs improves • the pudding; in that case use only half a cup of milk. -

Kendal Pudding.—Take one teacupful of ground rice, grated breadcrumhs. chopped suet, currants and sugar, one e2g, spice to taste, a little candied, peel chopped fine. and -k cupful of stoned and chopped raisins. Thoroughly mix these ingredients, adding teaspoonful of baking powder, and boil in a well-greased basin 11 hours. Serve with wine sauce. '

All Three Pudding.—Three oz. currants, chopped apples, suet, grated breadcrumbs, castor sugar, and three eggs. leaving out .liie white. Mix well, and boil for two hours Serve with sweet sauce flavoured with brandy, if approved.

Manchester Pudding,—Three oz. bread crumbs, pint of rew milk, a strip of lemon rind, four eggs, 2oz. butter, sugar to taste, and three tablespoonfuls of brandy. Flavour the milk with the lemon rind by infusing it for an hour ; then strain it on the breadcrumbs and boil it for two or three minutes; add the eggs, leaving out the whites of 2. the butter, sugar, and brandy. Stir all...these ingredients yell together. Cover a piedish with puff paste. and at the bottom put a layer of any kind of jam ; pnitr over it the mixturp, cold,, and bake thg pudding for one hour. Serve cold, with a little sifted sugar sprinkled over it.,

Snonste Cake Puddirg.—Put a pint of new rn'lk irto an enamel-liaed saucepan ; add a little salt and four talmimorfuls of sugar; then &x three tablespoonfuls of flour in cup of cold milk and stir in when the milk is scalding not. Stir it till it thickens, and let it conk for en minutes ; add the yolk of one egg well beaten ; rook five minutes, and remove from the fire. Slice some stale sponge cakes of an inch thick. Put a few spoonfuls of the custard in the bottom of a flat dish, and next A layer of cake spread thinly with jam ; then another layer of cake. and pour over it the remainder of the custard, which should have been kept warm. Beat the white of the egg to a stiff froth; add two tablespoonfuls of icing sugar, and beat till smooth ; then put it over the top of the custard. Set the dish in a tin plate half full of water, and put in a hot oven for five minutes to brown the frosting. Serve cold.

Cumberland Pudding.--Two ez. butter. 4oz. castor sugar, 3 eggs, 2oz. flour, 4oz. bread- crumbs. 1 gill of milk, 3oz. candied lemon peel. 3oT. dried cherries, a little essence of lemon, and some apricot, jam. Beat the butter to a cream, add the sugar, then the eggs one at a time ; stir in the flour and breadcrumbs, the milk, mix gently, add essence of lemon, candied peel (finely chopped) and the cherries. Pour the mixture into a buttered mould and steam for one and a-half hours. Dissolve 'tall a pot of apricot jam in hot water; turn the pudding on to a dish, and pour the sauce .over it, and serve at once.

Chocolate Pudding.—Half pound of light /tale breadcrumbs. 1 pint of new milk, loz. butter, 2 large teaspoonfuls of grated chocolate, 2 eggs well beaten, and a little sugar. Boil the brqati in the milk till it becomes a thick paste; when it has. cooled a little, add the other ingredients, and put the whole into a well-buttered piedisn. sake it for threequarters of an hour, and serve either with cream or cup custard.

Polished Floors.—When staining floor don't forget that the stain should be applied with the grain of the wood—that is, up and down the boards, not across. In polishing afterwards follow the same plan.

To Make Stockings Wear Well.—When knitting socks or stockings remember that a strand of silk knitted into the toes and heels with the wool makes them wear far better than if the wool is knitted alone.

Stains on Carpets.—Grease or blacklcad stains on carpets can be removed by covering them with a paste made of fuller's earth and Ivater to which either a hale spirits of tur- pentine or ammonia has been added. Leave till dry, and then brush.

A Mc.‘st Useful Hint in Dressmaking.—Lightly sap the seams of your skirt before ironing them instead of damping them, which is apt to stretch the cloth. Also, when putting new braid on a skirt, da.mp it first, then iron it, as this prevents shrinkage.

A Cure for Toothache.—Here is a very simple cure which a correspondent assures me is very efficacious. Saturate a small piece of cotton wool with glycerine and rose-water mixed in equal proportions. Put this in your ear, ands in a few minutes the pain will dis- a:Mear.

MOTHER'S IMBOUNKO JOY

TO every wife this extremely interesting • story of fond hopes thrice shattered, anclending in joy and happiness, cannot fail to prove of the utmost ,interest. It is the story of Mrs. S. E. Sarson, of Great Dalby, near Melton Mowbray, Leicestershire. "It was in 1904," says Mrs. Sarson, "that. I first experienced the hope and joy that come to the expectant mother. Despite every care I took of myself, I could not I ward off the pain and distress that come to so many women at such times, and I suffered with severe hLadaches and back- aches, dizziness and fainting. These symptoms of kidney disorder were bad enough but more alarming were the signs of dropsy that appeared. I was badly swollen under eves and my hands swelled. The water round my heart however, alarmed me most, for it caused such a heavy throbb- ing and beating. The doctor's medicine seemed powerless. • " In December, 1904, my first baby was prematurely born, and it lived but two short weeks. In the following year I went through a similar distressing experience, the same signs of kidney disease and dropsy Showing themselves. In . December, 1905, my second baby was born, also pre- maturely; and it, too, lived only two weeks. •" Yet again I passed through a like ordeal, suffering in the same way, and receiving medical treatment as on the two previous occasions, until, in August, 1906,

SA.RAti E. SAk'SON

(From a Photo),

WHO

my third baby was born—dead. Thus in Three years we had lost three children. "We gave up all hope of ever rearing. children: but later on I read the case of Mrs. Lewis, 51, Grove-terrace, Penarth, Cardiff, whose experience had been so like mine that I determined, should it be my lot to go through another trial, I would use no other medicine than Doan's Backache Kidney Pills, which had saved Mrs. Lewis and her children.

"When I found I was again to become a mother I started taking Doan's Backache Kidney Pills regularly. They kept my' kidneys well and they kept me well all through ; and, on 16th June, 1907, my fourth baby was born—healthy, strong: and well. Not once during my las; experence did I have the least sign of backache, head- ache, or dizziness, and I never fainted at all. There was not the slightest sign of dropsy; I was perfectly well all through, and I know that my own good health and the life and health of my baby are due to Doan's Backache Kidney Pills alone, for I used no other medicine during the whole time—and required none.

"(Signed) S. E. SARSON."

D.Jan's Backache Kidney Pills are 2s. 9d. per box (6 boxes for 135. 9d.) Of all Chemists and Stores, or post free, direct from Foster-McClellan Co., 8, Wells-Street, Oxford-street, London, W. (Use the full name—Doan's Backache Kidney Pills.)

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS WARRINGTON CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETY.

Lectures at the Parr Hall.

The Warrington Co-operative Society has been making rapid strides of late in the direction of provid- ing wholesome recreation for its members and so highly have these efforts been appreciated that on Saturday the Committee felt justified in making a new departure and engaging the Parr Hall for entertainments for both the children and the adults. In the afternoon a large gathering of children assembled for a cinematograph show which gave them great delight. The princip:ll series of views depicted the world's greatest slate quarries at Port Dinorwic, and there was also a host of humorous pictures which proved particularly accept- able. Mr. Toni Franks, of St. Helens, added to the afternoon's enjoyment with several ventriloquial sketches which created great merriment. In the evening there was an attrnction for the mlnits in the shape of an illustratel lecture entitled" fhrongh Sweden to Lapland, 1907," by Mr. H. Hibbert, F.R.G.S. Considering the very nominal charges for admission and the excellence of the lecture, one might well have expected a larger audience but, as it was, the attendande was encouraging. Mr. NATHAN COOK presided and in introducing the lecturer said that during the last three or four months the Co-operative movement had still further developed in Warrington. They had started supplying coal and they hoped to he able to supply their members with the best house coal they could possibly get. If they undertook anything they .could keep at it. They were not afraid of being bankrupt. (Laughter.) They bad no bankrupt stock, hut it was all of the hest quality and they could rely on it. They had opened a new boot stores in Warrington and he hoped the members would welcome it RS they could supply hoots from any maker the purchaser desired. They would do their best to please everybody in every direction. (Applause.) Mr. HtneEnT said he was not altogether pleased with the attendance. The night before he was at Bolton and one of the biggest halls in the neighbonrhood was packed with an audience of over 5,000, • The lecturer took the audience in imagination to Gothenburg and showed the view of a station which he said was under the control of the State. It would he a great blessing 'to England if our railways were under the State. ! Nothing brought that to his mind more i clearly than the proposed great strike of few weeks ego when he read a statement made by Mr. I Bell. M.P.. to the effect thas 50,000 railway men were working for less than a sovereign a week, and yet had wives and families to maintain. Tn Sweden 75 per cent, of the railways belonged to the State, nrui third. I class passengers received as much attention as the first- class passengers, but that was not the case in England. I A view of the opera hootre at Stockholm was thrown on the screen, and Mr. Flibbert expressed the opinion that if they wished the stage to he ours it must he pet, under the care of the municipnlity or the State. (Applause.) Commenting on the unemployed question. Mr. Hibbert said that if two situations could be found for each person In Warrington, they would still have some people out of work. There were many people who I were unemployable. He contended that our telephonic I system was too dear. The average cost in I England was ten gnineas ; in Sweden where the system was under tho control of the State, it was two guineas. It would never be cheaper until the vested interest. was I removed from the Home of Commons. (Applause.) I A view of the Town Hall of Stockholm was shown and the lecturer pointed out that when they bad any open spaces in Sweden they made them look beautiful, and did not stick big boards all the way round them. (Applause.) The lecture was highly interesting throughout and was beautifully illustrated, some of the most interesting views showing sports in progress. A solo wa: rendered very tastefully by Mr.P. Willett

Shorthand Writers' Association.

The second half of this Association's programme for the session which ends at the beginning of April, was entered into on Wednesday evening. when Mr. H. H. E. Hewitt, the Secretary of the St. Helens S.W.A., gave a demonstration on the " Tonch Systetn of Tyne writing," at the Wrinmarleigh Cafe, before a fairly large auditory.— Mr. Hewitt explained that this method of operating the typewriter was called the "touch system," because in writing with the machine one relies for the mechanical portion nr the work on the sense of touch—using each finger for certain keys in the manipulation. The way the system may he learned WAS lucidly exnlainokl. and also described on the blackboard, b " emin eon " machine afterwards being skflfully operated to show the advmtages of the system. Several of the mernl.4rs took part in the .übsequent (Emission on the lecture, to which Mr. Hewitt replied ; a hearty vote of thanks to him terminating a very ir.struction meeting.—Next week, Mr. G. J. Brown, F.1.P.5., the President of the Association, will give a lecture on" Shorthand as a hobby—and otherwise," when a good gathering of members and others interested is anticipoted.

===== 0002601_19080111_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
MUNICIPAL COAL. COUNCILLOICSTO—TT'S BOLD PROPOSITION. Council Rejects it.

The most important subject under discussion at the meeting of the Warrington Town Connell on 'Tuesday evening was that of a proposed municipal coal supply. Councillor Stott introduced the onestion in the-follow- ing motion :—" in the opinion of this Couneil it would be in the interests of the community for the Municipal Corporations to he granted powers to undertake the supply of coal to the inhabitants of the various boroughs and in order to further this nhiPet the Town Clerk 1-ne hereby instructed to circularize each town and city coilncil in Great Britain, asking them to pass a similar resolution, and to forward a cony of the same to the Association of Municipal Corporations,with a request that the association shall urge the Govern- ment to pass legislation giving powers to Municipal Corporations to undertake the sale of coal, and the purchase of coal mines." In the course of an interesting speech, Councillor STOTT said be trusted the Council would consider this question without prejudice, and forget. if they could, for the moment that it was Councillor Stott, the Socialist, who was submitting the motion, and discuss it from a business point of view, and from the stand- point of public interest. Those who had taken a keen interest in municipal work would have noticed the success of what were usually called municipal trading undertakings. Almost every person they met would agree with the municipality owning the gas and water supplies, now that they bad proved so very successfol, but many of them were inclined to disagree when they wanted to extend that principle to other commodities. If it was right for the Corporation to purchase thou- sands of tons of coal per year and sell it to the people in the form of gas, why should they not buy thonsands of tons more and sell it to the citizens as fuel? He was

CONVINCED THAT IT WOULD YE SUCCESSFUL, and would result in a great saving, not only to the idomestic consumer, but also to the manufacturers. Let him give them an instance. One of the committees of that Corporation had, within the last few weeks,accepted a tender for best house coal at 14s. per ton, and the contract was for a comparatively small supply. The domestic consumers were now paying 205. per ton. The only difference in the method of supply was that it MIS delivered to the Corporation by the ton, and to the householder by the hundredweight. If they added Is. per ton to the Corporation prices for plaoing the coal in cwt. bags, which he was informed was a fair price, they found that the domestic consumers were paying ss. per ton pore for coal than the Corporation. I What did that represent? They learnt from official reports that there were approximately 14,000 house- holders in Warrington. A fair average to take as the weekly consumption per householder would he 2cwt.l That meant that the domestic consumers in Warrington required each year about 73,000 tons of coal, for which they HAD TO PAY 218.250 MORE

than they would do if they could purchase at the same rate as one of their smallest coal consuming depart- ments. That was equal to a rate of Is. 7d. in the £ If they were to increase the rates Is. 7d. what would happen ? He was afraid some of them would net be able to meet their constituents at the next election. (Laughter.) It would also be to the interests of the manufacturers to he supplied through the municipality. The only argument he had hear(' advanced in favour of keeping the Corporation prices secret was because the local manufacturers had to pay more per ton for their coal than the Corporation paid, and the coal merchants did not wish the manufacturers to know the difference in the charges. If they were to purchase in very large quantities they could afford to pay for the services of a commercial chemist to test the quality of the coal. It was impossible under present cooditions for the domestic consumers to rely upon getting what they paid for. Better financial results even than those he had referred to could he obtained by municipalities owning their own mines. If it was not practical for one Corporation to

OWN COAL MINER, there was no reason why they could not co-operate with other boroughs. They were told a few months ago by Mr. Alderman Evans that the increased cost for the gas department over last year's prices was 2s. 6d. per ton. rt had been said that the high prices were caused by the high wages paid to the coal miners, who, they were told. were earning wages equal to a town clerk's salary. (Laughter.) They must not believe it. It wa, only a fairy tale. He was informed by one of the most respected miners' agents in the West Lancashire mining district that previous to the recent advance of 5 per cent, per ton, the average increase per ton to the actual miner during the twelve months prior to that date was approximately an average of 2.4 d. per ton for the district, and that the total wages cost to the mine owner did not average more than 4d. per ton. Where, then was the difference between the 4d. extra cost per ton and the 2s. Grl. increased charges going to ? Certainly not to the miner. He had information also from the West Riding of Yorkshire regarding extra wages cost. and the figures were also identical with those he had just given the Council. If they were compelled to go down into the coal mine and win the coal necessary for their own household requirements only for one year, he ventured to say they would never again complain that coal miners were too well paid. If any section of the industrial 'community deserved their sympathies it was the coal miner, for his work was both laborious and dangerous, and he had to labour under most depressing conditions. He could

W SPEAK PROM EXPERIESCP., For six years he was employed in the coal mine, and for three of those years water WAS continuously pouring into the workings. Very often they were drenched before they had been down the mine half an hour. He had to toil day after day with no more clothing on than a shirt and a pair of clogs. His father and two of his brothers at the present time worked in the coal mine. He could therefore speak with some knowledge of the life of the coal miner. It was a well-known fact that many of the coal mines at the present time were more profitable than gold mines. He remembered some years ago Sir James Joicey, who was a large coal owner, in a letter to the " Times " newspaper stating that

===== 0002601_19080111_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PROFITS_ MADE DY ME COALOWNERS

increased from 6•1 millions in 1849 to4ol millions in 1900. The extra cost in 1900 over the previous year to the Glasgow Corporation was El 66,250,t0 the Manchester Gas Department nearly £lOO.OOO, and it was estimated that the extra cost to the citizens of Bradford in that year was not less than £375,000—a sum almost equal to the whole of the local revenue from the rates. It was quite safe to say that the citizens of Warrington were fleeced to a similar extent. The only way in which they could prevent a recurrence of tho%e con- dition was by the people owning and working the coal mines in their own interests. Was there a single mem- ber of that Council who was prepared to say that coal (which no man placed into the bowels of the earth) should be monopolized for the personal profit of a few individuals? In his opinion coal was, or ought to be, the people's heritage. Coming to the second part of his motion, it was no USA submitting a resolution asking that this Corporation should decide to undertake a municipal coal supply. They had no power to do that, except by promoting an Act, and getting it through Parliament, which would be very expen- sive. That fact would have helped to defeat his object. By adopting the resolution they were, in his opinion, taking the best course, and certainly Ithe least expensive. By the co-operation of other boroughs acting through the Association of Municipal Corporations, he was confident that legislation would be influenced. The continual

GROWTH OF MUNICIPAL EXPENDITURE made it imperative that they should seek new sources of revenues. What member of the Council would say that municipal enterprise had reached finality? He believed it had scarcely yet begun. The principle of "what the people socially need they should socially own " would continue to grow. It was because he had a profound belief in the justice and practicability of that principle that he submitted the resolution. They, RS a Council, by passing the resolution, had a chance of making history which they, end those who followed them, would have cause to be proud of. He, therefore, asked the Council to pass the resolution unanimously, so that their action might be as influential as it was possible for them to make it.

===== 0002601_19080111_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CAUSE OF HIGH PRICES.

Councillor HAWTHORN said be seconded the motion in order that the Council might have an opportunity of discussing and expressing an opinion upon the subject. The motion might be a little before its time but it was one which deserved serious consideration from any municipality which professed to look after the interests of the community. At the present moment the whole community was feeling the effect of the recently enhanced price of coal. Every department of the Corporation was paying more for its coal, and when their balance sheets came to be made out they would probably find that the increased coal bill had reduced the profits of industrial undertakings. The cause of the increased cost of coal was not far to seek. The remission of the duty on exported coal had given an impetus to the export trade, and coalowners were taking advaniage of the depleted state of the market to raise latices all round. They were endeavouring to repeat the achievement of a few years ago when they succeeded in greatly enriching them- selves at the expense of the whole community. Coal was such a daily necessity of life that the question arose whether such an important commodity should be THE SPORT OF A COMP tIitTIVELY FF:W. INDIVIDUALS for private gain. There was a simmering of discontent at the present price of coal during this severe winter, and consumers were looking round for methods of checkmating the action of the coalowners, before whom even wealthy Corporations like their own were power- less to prevent a manipulation of prices to the detri- ment of the ratepayers. Drastic remedies had been suggested, and the motion of Mr. Stott was one of them. That motion would have served a useful purpose if it pointed a warning to the members of the Coal- owners' Association that these repeated exactions from the public could not be continued with impunity. He was aware that some members of the Council would

have nothing to do with the motion because it bad the taint of Socialism, but in the words of a distinguished statesman, "We are all Socialists now,"—(bear, hear)—and he con- fessed the word had no terrors for him. Self- interest would, of course, weigh against the proposal. Personally, be did not think that the distributors of coal were getting much of the enhanced price. The bulk of it was going to the owners of mines and royalties. ' Many years would elapse ere Municipal Corporations would have their own coal supply, but that motion was a significant hint to the private owners of mines that the community had a remedy in its hands if prices were advanced beyond reasonable limits. In supporting a motion of that kind he believed the Town Council was only pointing the way towards a solution of a state of things which was being felt by every householder and manufacturer at the present time, and which might become more pressing in the near future.

===== 0002601_19080111_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
NO NEW PRINCIPLE.

Councillor BALL, in supporting the motion, said he was hoping the opponents of the proposal wonld state their objections. He thought that, in view of the severe weather, the Council had to admit that this WAS a burning question. (Laughter.) The resolution offered no new principle whatever. It was a suggested exten- sion of a principle they had already admitted in many departments of their municipal life ; and when they wished to extend the idea of municipal ownership, control and distribution to coal the step was a very small one. Coal itself was a natural gift placed in the earth by the beneficient hand of Providence and if its beneficence had been marred by the mining owner and the royalty owner it was no reason why they should not take steps to minimize to some extent a glaring injustice. By taking that step they would relieve the rates to a far greater extent than they did at present by the manufacture and sale of gas. If the Council did not pass the resolution now they would be lagging behind in a very important and I pressing reform which would sooner or later be I.ronght forward by some other Corporation in the interests of the general body of the people.

===== 0002601_19080111_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
"WOULD INVOLVE MILLIONS."

The MAvott said the resolution was a most impor- tant one, and in his opinion it would involve the Council in an expenditure of millions of money. The Corporation could not even supply themselves that day from one or two coal pits for special purposes. There- fore if the resolution had to be carried out they would have to have different coal pits. Some gentlemen in Warrington started a coal pit at great cost and it had gone. The Whitecross Company had a pit to sapply themselves with coal, hut that had gene also. Other people had coal pits and could not make a profit. It was beyond the power of the Council to enter into the gigantic scheme proposed. Their own water works had not paid since they had them. (Cries of " Oh, oh.") Well, continued the Mayor, they were not miming a profit. The TOWN CLERK : We have made no loss. The MAYOR: Well, we have just cleared ourselves. NOT A DEBATING SOCIETY.

Alderman TINNION said that if that were a debating society instead of a Town Council the position the speakers had taken up would be an admirable one, but they had met that evening as business men to discuss business matters and not debating society pious opinions. Nor was the present the proper time to dis- cuss the question, especially as they had not had suffi- cient information to warrant them in passing the resolution of the kind proposed. But, apart from that, he objected to furthering particular views which would have such an extraordinary influence upon the future of Warrington in that particular way. Let them discuss something more concrete. He objected on principle to discussing mere principles when no good could be done by it. The case had been carefully and judicioasly laid before the Council, but it seemed to him that it had been brought forward in a purely academic sort of way. COUNCILLOR WILKINSON FAVOURS A MUNICIPAL COAL YARD.

Councillor WILKINSON said that when he first read the resolution he thonght the idea WWI that the Cor- poration might extend their fuel purchases and open a municipal coal yard to supply the inhabitants at a cheaper rate than they obtained it from private firms. He hid no objection to that, if they had the power. If, as a Corporation, they could buy large quantities of coal over and above what they required for their own manufactarine purposes, they would be able to sell coal to the inhabitants at a cheaper rate. They supplied them with coke now from the gas works. I: they had a municipal coal supply they would make a handsome profit which would go to the reduction of rates, and they would be able to supply the householder at cheaoe prices. Up to that point, he supported the resolution A PLEA. FOR THE COAL INTEREST.

Councilior ItoGERs asked, if they attempted to do what Councillor Wilkinson suggested, who was going to compensate the men who made their living out of the coal trade to-day--the coal dealers and hawkers ? Were they to he sent to the Workhouse ?

===== 0002601_19080111_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
COUNCILLOR WILSON "FAVOURABLY INCLINED."

Councillor WILSON said that when they considered that the Corporation were paying only 1-is, per ton for coal and the poor people were charged £l, it made them pause and think, especially at this time of the year, what was to become of the poor people ? It was a great consideration to have to pay 25 per cent, more by purchasing in small quantities. With regard to Councillor Rogers' remarks, the coal proprietors and dealers had made a fair pile out of the coal during the the last few years. (Laughter.) Consideration should be extended not to the men who had made vast fortunes and did very little with them but to the people who were toiling to make a living, There were hundreds of houses that night without fires. Ile was very favourably inclined to Councillor Stott's motion and if they could supply coal a 14s. per ton it would be better for the inhabitants of the town at large.\ _

===== 0002601_19080111_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
"WAIT FOR CHEAPER 4LLIERIES."

Councillor NMI) said he was very much in sympathy with Councillor Stott in his eeeire to provide the poor with cheaper coal, but he did not believe that if the Corporation undertook to supply coal to the community the Council would get the privileged prices they enjoyed to-day. If Councillor Stott set his heart upon any particular colliery which was a profitable concern, the price that would be asked would place it absolutely beyond i their reach. Before they could become purchasers they would have to find a seller, and that was not always an easy matter. If there was any argu- ment in fettour of Councillor Stott's resolution that the Council should at any time put itself in possession of a colliery, the present was not the time. It would be better to wait until the collieries were cheaper— (laughter)—and coal was at a lower price.

===== 0002601_19080111_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
COUNCILLOR STOTT'S REPLY.

I Ceeneill-c;r STOTT briefly replied, and, in answer to Alderman Tinnion. said be could not imagine a better way than the resolution for bringing the matter before the Council. It wonid be no use to ask the Council to undertake the municipal coal supply at once,hecause they had not power to do so. When would he the proper time to bring the matter forward ? Why was it not the proper time now? Alderman TINNION : I never said it was not the proper time. I said we were not here to discuss pious opinions, and that what was proposed was not the business way. COnneillor STOTT assured the Conncil that be sincerely believed in the resolution and had advocated these principles for the last ten years. He thought he had proved that the resolution would be beneficial. Some of the members seemed to think that if the resolution were carried the General Purposes Committee would immediately appoint a committee to consider the purchase of a coal mine—(laughter)—bnt nothing could be more absurd. He did not expect if the reso— lution was passed that even with the assistance of other Corporations the Association of Municipal Cor- porations would decide to petition Parliament upon the question at present, but it was time to make a com- mencement and in a few years public opinion would probably be sufficiently strong to influence Parliament to legislate on those lines. No Corporation would attempt to buy coal mines at the present high prices. He was more concerned about the difference of ss. per ton between the price naid by the Corporation and the price paid by domestic consumers. He thought that from £16,000 to £19,000 a year might be saved to the inhabitants and if that was not business hs did nut know what was. The voting resulted as follows : FOR THE MOTION. AGAINST. Councillor Stott Alderman Tinnion Wilkinson „ Burton Barton „ Forshaw Skelland Evans 9, Ball Councillor Evans Hawthorn-6 „ Sankey Nield „ Rogers Singleton

Roberts and the Mayor—fl The MAYOR declared the motion lost.

When Mr. ASQUITH pro- OLD-AGE duces his Old-Age Pension PENSION scheme his most well' SCHEMES. prepared critic will probably

be Mr. GEORGE N. BARNES, who is Among those who have been suggested as successor of Mr. KIER-HARBIE in the leadership of the Independent Labour Party in the House of Commons. Mr. BARNES, who is the chief official of the strongest of our trade unions, has given much study to the question, and we venture to predict that whatevar Mr. Asouun's scheme may be he will not agree with it. A universal scheme, Mr. BARNES thinks, is the only one which would leave voluntary associations of wage-earners to pursue their bepeficent course undisturbed by Government interference. He is, that is to say, anxious in the first place lest the provident portion of the work of the trade unions should be impaired. Few members of the public are aware of the great extent to which the trade unions relieve the rates. Mr.BAnDrEs's own organization . the Amalgamated Society of Engineers, is at present supporting over 5,000 of its old members by means of pensions ranging from 7s. to 108. a week. The cost of doing this amounts to close on £120,926, most of which sum would have had to be found by the ratepayer if what the late Lord SALISBURY w•mid have called a "cruel organisa- tion " had not acted honestly and generously to its old members.

The arguments which Mr. BARNES may be expected to advance in the House of Commons when the CHA.NCELLon of the EXCHEQUER pro- duces his proposals may be safely anticipated, for a recent lecture of his on this subject has been rinted and circulated as a pamphlet. His con- tention is that schemes which involve discrimina- tion between those to whom pensions are given will work out to the benefit of the worthless, and not of the worthy ; that the contributory principle is inapplicable; and that pensions must be given as a civil right in order to be acceptable to the honest and sensitive poor. We believe we may assure Mr. BARNES that the coatributory prin- ciple, which is a feature of most Old-Age Pension systems, including that of Germany, will form no part of Mr. AsQUFTH'S plans. SI far, therefore, there can be no conflict. It is equally certain, however, that the CHANCELLER oF THE EXCHEQUER will find himself in no position to propose a universal scheme of Old-Age Pensions. Mr. BARNES himself admits that to pay ss. a-week to everybody who is seventy years of age would cost nearly £16,000,000 a-year, and that it would require £10,000,000 more to begin the pensions at sixty-five. Mr. Arson= is a man of much courage and daring; but he would turn pale, we imagine, if he had to look these figures in the face in con- nection with his next Budget. His ,proposals, it may be taken for granted, will be based on the sound principle that we must crawl before we run. Mr. BARNES, it is true, makes a large speculative reduction of the actual amount that would be needed to work a universal pension system. After certain deductions were made, the cost on a seventy years age basis would, he thinks, be between £7,000,000 and £8,000,000 and about £15,000,000 on a sixty-five years basis. And how are the deductions to be made? It is taken for granted that under a universal system large numbers of persons of good position would not claim their pensions, and that in this way a large amount of money would be saved to the State. This is no doubts perfectly safe assumption, but it is scarcely agreeable to find it reckoned upon as part of an Old-Age Pension scheme; for this, surely, would be charity—a scornful charity, indeed, but charity without doubt. We already pay, as Mr. BARNES points out, about £10,000,000 a year in pensions. We could never save this sum; but those amOrig whom it is divided would be excluded from the new pension system. Then there are the inmates of the work- houses and the pauper lunatic asylums. These would still further reduce the number of claimants; and still, as Mr. BARNES admits, it would take, rnakirg every possible or imaginary deduction,' £15,000,000 a year to provide a pension of ss. a week for all persons of sixty-five years of age or over. We may safely assume that Mr. Aswan; will try something more experimental.

Aldermanic changes are THE NEWEST taking place with unusual ALDERMAN. rapidity in the Warrington Town Council. A change in most things is usually considered beneficial and a re-shuffling of aldertnanic seats is a good thing every now and then. The only cause for regret is that lately death has been the prime mover in the 1 changes which have taken place. The position rendered vacant by the death of Alderman Fair- dough has been filled by the elevation of Coun- cillor Thomas Barton, whom we have much pleasure in congratulating on a unanimous vote. Alderman Burton has been a member of the

Council continuously since 1896 and daring that period ho has faithfully represented St. Austin's Ward, and served with conspicuous assiduity on six committees of one of wnich—the Health Committee —he is now deputy-chairman. Alderman Burton has always been one of the quiet members of the Council, but his sterling character for uprightness, and manufacturing experience, have always assured him a respectful I hearing whenever he has intervened in debate. It is rather curious that, notwithstanding the changes, the aldermanic bench is no younger in point of individual age. The latest additions, Aldermen Ellison and Burton are two of the most silvery-haired and venerable of the members of the Council. Fortunately, it is a pretty cnclusive theory that with age comes wisdom.

Owing to the appointment ST. AUSTIN'S WARD Of Alderman Berton there VACANCY, is, of course, a municipal vacancy in St. Austin's Ward. A candidate is already in the field in the person of Mr. John Maginn, who sat for St. John's Ward for the three years prior to 1906. Mr. j Maginn is standing as an independent candidate, and when last in the Council there was no doubt of his independence of spirit and action. He ' tackled what appeared to him to be public ' injustices with a truly Irish vigour and pugnacity, and his speeches were always enlivened with native wit. Tt, is understood that .he will be opposed by Mr. J. Broadhurst an officialn Conservative candidate. We hope Mr. Magin will be returned on the 20th by a good majority, for there is a place for him on the Warroagtou Town Council if only as an unflinching opponent of the brewing interest.

Councillor Stott frightened MUNICIPAL COAL. the Town Council on Toes-

day, when he proposed his resolution in favour of the principle of municipal Corporations undertaking the supply of coal. As a body, the Town Council is easily frightened, arid when the scare is on it does not stop to think. The Mayor was the first member to use the voice of fear, and his example was followed by several Councillors with sheep-like equanimity. Said his Worship, in tones of deep concern, "the resolution would involve the Council in an expenditure of millions of money." Could any- thing be more absurd or indicative of thoughtless judgment ? All that the motion could commit the Council to was an expression of opinion, and the forwarding of a resolution to the Association of Municipal Corporations. Councillor Stott purposely couched his proposal in moder- ate terms, being fully aware that its Socialistic character would arouse prejudices which are perfectly impervious to logic, and he made out a very good case for the ultimate municipalisation of the coal supply. It is unfortunate for the town that so many Councillors cannot see any further than their noses. They jumped to the conclusion that if the resolution were carried the Corporation would have to forthwith go into a very dear and deadly hostile market, purchase a coal-mine or two sind commence at once retailing coal to the community. Even the Socialists who do some- times ride row2hshod over obstacles, would not be guilty ot formulating such a fatuous proposal. The arguments of the Mayor and Councillor Nield simply begged the question and reflected very unfavourably on their capacity as administrators of a municipality. Alderman Tinnion's lofty view that they were there " to discuss business matters and not pious opiniops" was also lacking in wisdom. Altogether, the Town Council was seen, in a very poor light, as it usually is when there ia some question on the minutes necessitating a wide outlook. . _

We --egret that the resolution was not carried, land we regret even more that it was not discussed on its merits. Messrs. Stott, Hawthorn and Ball—who all belong to the advanced school of thought—spoke with logical force and directness in favour of the municipalisation of the coal-supply as a vital reform at which the Council should aim. They did not ask that the principle of" what the people socially need they should socially own" might be confirmed. The time is not ripe for such a proposal, but they are quite within their rights and reason in suggesting that the Corporation which has municipalised the supply of gas, water, coke and elec- tricity might approve of the first steps for adding coal to the list. Not oven the most conservative of Councillors can contend that the coal-supply to the ordinary consumer is on a fair basis, or that its municipalisation does not offer a prospect of cheaper coal—a boon almost as great as cheap food. If the supply of bread was in the hands of capitalists to the sarne extent, if prices were manipulated for selfish ends and over-weighted with royalties as coal-prices are, there would soon be such an outcry that the Corporations would be compelled to act at once in defence of the corn • rnunity. As representatives of the people why should they be content to sit supinely while the coal-owners squeeze the public? As a big and valued customer the Corporation can buy the best coal at 14s. per ton, while the poor private consumer has to pay 205., and has no safeguard that he is even getting what he has. paid for. If the municipalities tackled the question there is no doubt that they could mine and supply coal profit- ably at a far less figure. But they would have to act in concert in order to fight successfully the huge vested interests which batten on th 3 coal trade, and the first step—which will have to be taken sooner or later—will need to be on the lines suggested by Councillor Stott. Warrington might have had the honour of leading the way to a great social reform, and of helping to make history, but apparently the Council prefers to wait for others to move and to then trot along humbly in the rear.

All who desire the uplifting THE AI,ITIT of the masses will cordially- SonooL. wish success to the War- rington Adult School which has just been started under encouraging auspices. In , other parts of the kingdom these institutions have obtained a strong hold and are doing a great work in helping people to make the best use of their lives. As the , enterprise is new to this district, it may be well to explain that an adult school is a society of men or women (over seventeen or eighteen years of age) formed for the purpose of mutual helpfulness. The basis of an adult school is the practical teaching of Jesus Christ. It does not concern itself with the spread- ing of special theoriss, but aims at helping the members in their actual lives. The free, but reverent and practical, study of the Bible con- ducted in common, with full opportunity for dis- cussion, is deemed the centre oh the school work. Associated with each School will be found some or all of the following :—Library, Savings Bank, Night School, Lectures and S icial Evenings, Technical Classes, Club Rooms, Sick Benefit, Card, Book, and Athletic Clubs, etc. Each school is governed as much as possible by the members themselves. The only qualification for membership is a wish to join the School. The members of a School may belong to any denomintttion or to none. The Warrington school has already attracted an energetic band of workers and all who feel anxious to assist in the movement will be cordially welcomed at the British Workmen's Coffee House, Horseniarket-street, at 8.45 any Sunday morning. The school closes sufficiently early to enable its members to attend morning service at a place of worship if they so desire.

Only last week we had Ouu ARMY occasion to comment upon VETERANS. the thoughtlessness and ingratitude of a nation which allows its old soldiers to spend the close of their days in poverty and destitution. The I thought was suggested by the presence at the Old Folk's Treat of a contingent of army veterans, several of whom had come from the Workhouse, which is now their only home. The Mayor rightly emphasised. the need for making some adequate Provision for the men who served their country with the colours, and Commended the efforts which have been supported locally to attain that end. We are pleased to see that a national move- ment is now on foot, and thanks are due to Lord Roberts for taking the lead in the matter and issuing a powerful appeal to the public. He points out that strong feeling has been aroused in many quarters that it is in every way discreditable to our country

that the present state of things should be possible, and it is most desirable that an effort should be made without delay to remedy it. Toe movement has the entire approval of his MF.jesty the King, who has graciously consented to become the patron of The Veterans' Relief Fund," and to open the list of subscribers with the donation of 1,000 guineas. It is proposed to collect a sufficiently large sum to ensure every Crimean or Indian Mutiny veteran beiog released from the workhouse and then to make him such a weekly allowance as ! will enable the old sailor or soldier to spent his remaining d tys in modorate comfort and respoot- ability,lor ensure solue amelioration of his circum stances if from age or infir:nity he is obliged to remain in the worknoirie infirmary. Contributions may be sent either to the hon. secretary and treasurer, Colonel [furlock Pritchard, C. 5.1., Eaglesdeld, Camberley, Surrey; or to Lord Roberts at Engletnere, Ascot.
