NOTES AND NOTIONS.

" What is an epistle ? " was the question recently put at an adult school, and the reply came promptly The wife of an apostle." _ _

There was once a playwright who sat in the front row at the first night of a new piece of his own. The piece failed. It failed dreadfully. As the playwright sat, pale and sad, amid the hisses, a woman behind him leaned forward and said : " Excuse me, sir • but, knowing you to be the author of this play, I took the liberty, at the beginning of the performance, of snipping off a lock of your hair. Allow me now to return it you." . . * . .

A clergyman in the itanclTester diocese, writing to another clergyman the other day, addressed his letter to " The Rev. B. A. --," and began, " Dear --, Forgive me for putting your degree where your initials should be, but I have forgotten your initials." In reply he received a letter addressed to " The Rev. --, MC.," which began," Dear, --, Forgive me for putting your initials where your degree should be. I have forgotten your degree." The writer of the first letter has no degree. * I A " Deceased Wife's sister Dance" was given at ' Manchester on Wednesday night, at the Corn Exchange. It appears that on the death of her sister, a lady went to keep house for the widower, and a mutual attachment springing up between them, the husband married her, vowing that if ever the Deceased Wife's Sister Bill became law he would celebrate the occasion. He never lived to see that event, and now his widow has realised his desires. *

With characteristic thoughtfulness, Mr. A. H. Crosfield, M.P., wrote to the Secretary of the War- rington Junior Liberal Association (Mr. H. W. Gueat) on the eve of the New Year, wishing them the cordial greetings of the season. In the course of his letter Mr. Crostield said :—" I hope it will be a year of happiness and prosperity for you all, and that as time goes on your association will exercise an ever- increasing influence on the political history -of our Borough." * * * The fund for the Liberal Party's wedding gift to the Borough Member is being enthusiastically sub- scribed to, and the lists will close on January 20th. We hope that those who possess the common failing of procrastination will at once forward their donations to either Mr. C. E. Parker, Alderman Tinnion, Mr. T. Pemberton or Councillor J. T. Locker. Mr. and Mrs. Crosfleld have promised to attend a reception in Parr Hall on Wednesday, February sth, when the presentation will be made. * The Local Government Board Inspector who visited the Warrington Poor Law Guardians on Wednesday had a little treat in store for those worthies. The Chairman cheerfully hinted that the Guardians liked flattery, but the inspector—hard-hearted man—did not see his way to oblige and delivered quite a remark- able homily in which he said some plain things about " tittle-tattle" and gossip The Guardians went away sadder and we hope all the wiser for a lecture which could not be considered out of place in view of recent events. * * * One of the most ludicrous features of the discussion about a municipal coal-supply at Tuesday's Council meeting was provided by Councillor Wilson, who spoke with unaccustomed warmth about the price of coal and the hardships of the poor then when the time came to vote for the resolution he was apparently in sympathy with, he sat mum. Not a single Tory was in favour of the resolution and perhaps Coun- cillor Wilson, NV ho is a valued appendage of the Tory party, discovered this fact during the period which elapsed between the conclusion of his speech and the taking of the vote. lie usually likes to go with the crowd, and on Tuesday the crowd sat tight. So did Councillor Wilson.

and

* * * Mr. F. Monks, J.P. is one of those captains of industry who have been closely associated with Warrington's progress and he is able to look back upon a strenuous and useful life of over seventy years spent in " the good old town." His retirement to Southport is regretted by his many friends and it was peculiarly fitting that the Warrington Society should entertain him at a complimentary dinner as he has been one of the foremost helpers of the Society. Mr. Monks made a highly interesting speech in the remin- iscent strain and he may be pardoned for feeling some little pride in the part he has taken in public activities, especially as the founder of that most beneficent institution, the Nursing Association. We join with the Warrington Society in wishing him a long and happy life in his new home. * * * The Mayor is determined to obtain those fifteen £2o's which are needed to clear off the debt on the District Nursing Association, and we would advise those who are well blessed with this world's goods .o pay up pleasantly at the earliest opportunity. His Worship has become a most energetic canvasser for the institution and is ever on the look-out for likely subscribers. At the dinner at the Old Academy on Thursday evening he had the pleasure of announcing that Mr. F. Monks and Mr. William Long had each promised him £2O and this was quickly followed by an offer by Mr. C. E. Parker to give £2O if the Mayor would become a member of the Warrington Society. Needless to say his Worship immediately closed with the offer. He is now on the look-out for five more subscribers to make up the £3OO.

STOCKTON HEATH GARDEN SOCIETY. Annual Meeting. ,

The annual meeting of the Stockton Heath Garden Society was held at the Hall Cafe, on Thursday, Mr. C. Plinston in the chair. Mr. Vere B. Davies, the president of the Society was also present. The Chairman in his opening remarks referred to the great damage that had been done to some of the trees and was very sorry indeed that they had not yet received any information as to who had done the damage. THE COMMITTEE'S REPORT. Mr. A. Fawcett, the secretary of the Society, then presented the annual report of the committee which stated that the Society was admittedly doing useful work in the district and generally stimulating its mem- bers to increased attention in the cultivation of their own particular gardens and to keen competition at the shows. Two additions to the Society during the year held promise of great usefulness in the near future, namely the Latchford Section and the juvenile branch and they trusted that their development might be as marked as that of the Parent Society and that their 'interest might increase and act as a stimulant to the Society generally. The report was adopted. RECEIPTS AND EXPENDITURE. The Secretary then presented the balance sheet which showed a balance of £1 12s. 6d. The subscriptions amounted to £63 14s. Gd., total receipts £123 18s. id. • expenditure £l3l 14s. 9d. There was a sum of £9 9s. 24 in hand from the previous year. ELECTION OF OFFICERS. The following were elected officers for the ensuing year:—President, Mr. Vero B. Davies ; Committee, Mr. C. Plinston chairman; Mr. W. H. Woodcock, vice-chairman; Messrs. Lawrence, Watson, Owen, T. Baxter, F. Broady, W. Downs, T. Ethlen, J. H. Fletcher, M. W. Grant, J. Hallows, W. Hickman, W. Leach,J. Moore,J. McDonald, J. Mynekyme, Baker, Evans, Picka.vance, Hudson, W. Skelton, F. Timperley, W. A. Timperley, E. Vernon, E. G. White ; Hon. Treasurer, Mr. C. Plinston, Hon. Secretary, Mr. A. Fawcett, and Mr. Downs, assistan secretary.

SPLENDID BcrrEß is. lb.—AsRLEv'S, Sankey st.—Advt. Amalgamated Musicians' Union. A branch of the above has been formed in War- l'ington with Mr. Tom Boothroyd as president and Mr. Davies, 142,Lovely-lane, as secretary. The first meeting took place on Sunday evening last at the Pelican Hotel, Butterrnarket-street, when about 40 members were enrolled including almost all the leading musicians of Warrington and the members of the orchestras from the local theatres and music halls. It has been felt for a considerable time that some organization was required by the local musicians where they could air their grievances and that some standard rate of remuneration should be fixed for the iconcert and ball business, thereby avoiding the cutting down of prices which has been so prevalent in the past An orchestra will be formed which will cater for concert, hall and garden party business and by affiliating the branch with the Trades and Labour Council they hope to receive the co-operation of all trade unionists. The bar was under the management of Mr. T. Hockenhall. Army Veteran who Likes the Workhouse. The " Daily Teleraph " publishes a collection of tstatistics compiled from official sources, which shows that nearly GOO veterans who risked their lives in the Crimea, the Indian Mutiny, and earlier wars are ending their lives as paupers in London and provincial workhouses. In only one single instance does a veteran express himself desirous of ending his days in the workhouse. This is the case of an old Royal Military gunner in his eighty-first year, who, writing from Warrington Union Infirmary, says :—" In this Institution (presided over, as it is, by one of the best masters in the kingdom), I have been an inmate for four years, and I do not see my way to accept anything involving a change. I should hie parting from a circle of dear friends, whose kindness and solace in my declining years I can iil afford to lose. . . . I can, there- fore, only express a fervent wish to remain where I

Cheshire Training College Extension. At a meeting of the Cheshire Education Committee at Crewe, on Xlmiday, it was stated that Lord Crewe had accepted the committee's offer of £6,00 for ten acres of land on his lordship's estate for a training college. The Board of Education suggested the acquisition of a further two acres, with a view to the extensrm of the college, but the committee did not regard this as desirable at present. Warrington Philomathic Society. A meeting of the above Society was held at Gregory's Cafe on Wednesday .evening. Mr. li. J. Burgess ppened a discussion upon the " Congo Question," and p )inted out that the Congo Reform Association had proved their case and had indisputable evidence to justify their statements. An interesting discussion followed, in which the majority of the members present tosk part. Unfortunately (from a debating point of view) no member had been able to procure information or discover arguments sufficiently convincing to cause any opposition to the general idea that Great Britain had a duty to perform in the matter and the members were unanimously of the opinion that the Congo Reform Association were right.

Union Bank of Manchester, Ltd.

The nett profits of the Union Bank of Manchester, Limited, for the half-year ended 31st December 1907, including £10,764 brought forward, amount to £57,046. The Directors hay,' declared the usual dividend of 12s. per share (free of income tax), absorbing £30,000 and have transferred £2,000 to promises reduction 'account, £2,500 to pension fund, and £5,000 to reserve. fund (raising that fund to £342,000), and have also placed £7,500 to Consols reduction account, reducing the Bank's Consols from 831 to 82. This leaves the amount to be carried forward at £lO,OlO. Death of Mrs. John Dewhurst. We regret to announce the death of Mrs. John Dewhurst, widow of the late Mr. John Dolby Dewhurst, of Oughtrington House, Lymm, which took place last Friday evening at her Bournemouth residence. Mrs. Dewhurst was the daughter of the Rev. W. Mac , Iver, a former rector of Lymm, and well known in the , district to the older inhabitants. Her husband was a member of the firm of Messrs. G. and R. Dewhurst, cotton spinners, of Great Marlborough-street, Manchester, and was most generous towards all good work in Lymm and Oughtrington. He gave several thousand pounds towards the re building of the Lynam Parish Church tower. The funeral took place at St. Peter's, Oughtrington, on Wednesday.

PUBLIC BENEFIT.-lINDER TIIE CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN LANCASFIIRIL—Advt. Mr. J. J. Bilsbrough's Retirement.

Some time ago the approaching retirement of Mr. J. J. Bilsbrough, goods agent and dockmaster of the London and North-Western Company at Widnes, was announced. Mr.Bilsbrough is a well-known resident of Latchford. His official career terminated with the close of the year, and to mark the occasion an interesting presentation took place in Lacy-street Schoolroom, Widnes, on Monday night, in the presence of the staff and district officials, when the district superintendent (Mr. H. S. Hunt), in appropriate terms, handed to Mr. 13ilsbrough a handsome gold watch, presented by the goods department staff as evidence of their goodwill and affection towards the recipient. Asked for More—And got it. _

Kate Layden, Ellesmere7street, a well-known War rington character, was charged at the Warrington Police Court, on Tuesday, with wilfully breaking a plate glass window, value £4 10s., at the shop of Ch,rles Price, provision dealer, Butterrnarket-street. On Monday Sergeant Barlow heard a smash of glass near Market-gate. On proceeding there he found the prisoner leaning against the shop window with her clog in her hand. The only explanation she gave for her conduct was that a man named Mullins had been locked up for stealing bacon from the shop, and she had done it for " revenge." The magistrates sentenced her to six weeks, but the prisoner clung to the dock rail and demanded to be sent away for four months, as Mullins received that term. The magistrates partially satisfied her desire by adding another two weeks to her sentence.

'LINSEED COMPOUND,' Trade Mark of Key' Compound Essence of Linseed for Coughs & Colds.--Ad vt P.S.A. Meetings.

At the meeting of the Wycliffe P.S.A. Society on Sunday Mr. W. Beswick presided and wishes' all Present "A very happy and prosperous New Year." The Rev. John Yonge (the president) gave the New Year's address upon "Christ became man for us. Let us be men for Christ." He *sad that the great mass of people believed and acknowledged the grandeur and the excellence of Christ's life upon earth. It was one of goodness, love, and activity. As a workman He gave His best, as a son He was filial awl obedient, and His works were full of purity and genuineness. It was not for Himself, but for man, He bought us to set us free. When a dark slave was bought by a Christian man for 1,450 dollars, and was told she was free, she was full of joy unspeakable, and said she would be his servant for ever. So let them all realize what it cost the Saviour to set them free. They must yield themselves willing servants to Him. Mr. Gleare and the Secretary took part in the tneeting, and Mr. A. Griffiths sang " If with all your hearts' in an effec- tive manner. Mr. W. H. Paytan presided at the organ. There are now 430 members. —An open meeting of the Buckley-street P.S.A. Society was held on Sunday under the presidency of Mr. W. Rogers, of Winwick. Mr. Stawe, of Widnes, was the speaker, and gave a very interesting address. Mr. J. Perrin ably rendered the tenor solo " Sabbath melodies." Miss L. Fairhurst read the afternoon's lesson, and Miss M. Plinston pre- sided at the piano. The collection was in aid of the 1 society's funds.

- Manchester and Liverpool District Banking Company. The directors of this company have just issued their ' report and balance sheet which will be presented at an ordinary meeting of shareholders to be held at the Head Office,Spring Gardens, Xfanchester, on Thurs- day the 16th inst. at 12 noon. They state that the gross profit for the half-year ending 31st December last, after providing for all bad and doubtful debts, is £311,134 7s. 2i1., to which has to be added the amount brought forward from 29th June last, viz. : £41,774 13s. 11d., making a total of £352,009 is. H. From this sum the following deductions have to be made : total expenses at the Head Office, London Office and Branches, including salaries, stationery, taxes, &c., £105,928 10s 6d. ; half-year's provision for Income Tax, £5,370 10s., leaving a net profit of £241,600 14s. 7d. The Directors recommend the declaration of a Dividend of 21s. per share, which will take £165,900, leaving a balance of 1.75,709 14s. 7d. Of this it is proposed to carry to the Reserve Fund £15,000; to Pension Fund £10,000; and to next account, £,..0,709 14s. 7d. The Reserve Fund, with the above addition, will then .amount to £1,685,000. The directors have the pleasure of intimating that the amalgamation of the Lancaster Banking Company Limited with this bank. as from Ist July last, has been satisfactorily completed. rho directors of the Lancaster Bank have joined the Board of this bank. During the past half-year offices have been opened at Halsall and Scarisbrick (Bescar-lane) as sub-branches to Ormskirk, and a branch is now open at Altrincham, with a sub-office at Hale.

Compensation for the Loss of a Husband. At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Shiress Will, N.C. the case of Mary Ellen Leigh, Lord Nelson-street, v. Ames Fairclough and Sons was down for hearing. Mr. C. H. Unsworth said he appeared for both parties. Mts. Leigh's husband met with an accident while in the employ of the firm in July and died in November. By an agree- ment between the parties the amount of compensation had been fixed at £253 19s. 6d. which had already been paid into court and ,he wished to ask the Judge to allocate die am ,uat in pane lies! payments to the widow. There was just one question as to whether one of the children —a youth of 19 —was a dependent. He (Mr. Unsworth) submitted that he was not and the youth himself was quite willing that the order should be made entirely in favour of the widow. —Samuel Lee was called, and said he was in receipt of wages of 14s. per week. He was willing that his mother should have the wMle of the money.—The widow stated that her son had been in regular work from the age of fourteen.—His Honour decided that the youth was not dependent upon his father at the time of his death, and made the order entirely in favour of the widow. He agreed to £2O being devoted to funeral expenses, &c, and directed the balance of the money to be paid in instalments of £2 per month. AN OBJECT LESSON.

American Newspaper Publishers

and the Tariff.

The newspaper publishers of America have made a great discovery. They are themselves, they find, the 1 victims instead of the beneficiaries of the protective system which has been ardently chanipioned in many of their editorial columns. They need no legislative defence for their own products against foreign com- petition, but the boons which they have helped the Government to confer upon their fellow-countrymen have become weapons for their own chastisement. A doleful complaint was made the other day, at a Chicago conference on trusts,by r. Herman Ridder,of the New York" Staats Zeitung." The cost of every article used by the publishing business has been subjected, he says, "to an artificial stimulation." The newspaper pro- prietors lie at the mercy of the big combinations which Dingleyism has fostered. They have a hundred million dollars invested in machinery, all unduly taxed by the manufacturer. They buy their type-setting machines from a single company and their advertising type from a trust. In some cities combinations of advertisers have succeeded in marking down their scale of rates.' Their industry, declares Mr. Riddler, is" loaded with the burdens arising from the Protection of every interest with which it deals." - The most severe pressure.of all is that which they endure from the International Paper Trust. The printing and publishing business as a whole turns over its capital in about ten months, and large department stores turn over their seven times a year, but the Paper Trust, with a capital exceeding sixty-one million dollars does a gross annual business of only twenty-one millions It has watered itself until it has no more money to invest,and it has borrowed upon everything it possesses. Instead of extending its business in the normal fashion of commercial growth, it has diminished its supply to such an extent that it turns out less news-print paper to-day than it did immediately after its organisa- tion. The market has been so starved that many news- paper proprietors are unable to get any quotations at all for their next year's white paper. The price for the present year has been advanced 12 dollars a ton, and an additional advance of 10 dollars is threatened for next year. The trust itself explains this rise as due to the growing scarcity of material, but somehow or other it has found itself in possession of a surplus product which it has been selling in this country at a cent a pound less than the American rate. It is further alleged that some of its methods in preventing competition deserve the attention of the Department of Justice. A Boston publisher attempted the other day to obtain a supply from Canada. He suddenly found, after he had completed his arrangements, that the mail was unable to do the work. Later, an agent of the trust recounted to him every step in his negotiations and laughed at his efforts to escare from its cinches. The menace to the newspaper interest has become so 'serious as to provoke a widespread revolt against the sacred schedules of the Dingley Tariff. A special meet- ing of the American Newspaper Publishers' Association, representing 278 newspapers published in 141 cities, has called for the exercise of existing statutes against trade combinations, for the punishment of" certain Western I mills " guilty of such combination, and, inost significant of all, for the immediate repeal of the duties on printing paper, wood pulp, and all other material entering into the manufacture of printed paper. These resolutions have been presented to Mr. Roosevelt by an influential deputation. He has promised to institute an investigation into the alleged breaches of the ' law, and to include in his annual Message to Congress, a strong recommendation for the relief sought for. Congress, however, is not accustdmed to give legislative effect to more than a small proportion of Mr. Roosevelt's suggestions,and there is little doubt that this exhortation will go the way of the rest. The managers of the Republican party are too shrewd to allow such an opening of the waters as would follow the removal of even one tariff barrier, and they will be able to quote the President's own understudy, Secretary Taft, in favour of the doctrine that there should be no attempt at revision until after the next Presidential election. Meanwhile the incident tends toward edifica- tion, if only as reminding us of the fact, too often over- looked, that the product of one manufacture may be to another the raw material on whose freedom from 1 harassing imposts the prosperity of a great industry depends.—" Manchester Guardian."

Dickinson, ALA., who was at one time a curate at the Warrington Parish Church and is now stationed at Holy Trinity Church, Southport, has accepted the living of Padgate, which ib in the gift of the Rector of Warrington. On his former residence here, he made him- self a general favourite, especially among the young men attached to the Warrington Parish Church School.

UNEMPLOYED PROBLEM.

A resolution was submitted from the Tottenham Urban District Council stating that the unemployed problem was of national concern and could be only satisfactorily dealt with on a national basis and calling upon the Government to deal with the matter by under- taking works of national necessity such as reaffore- station, reclamation of forshores and waste land and the institution of farm colonies &c. It was further agreed to petition Parliament in favour of Mr. Macdonald's Unemployed Workmen Bill 1907 subject to further financial responsibility being placed upon the local authorities administering the Act. The Council were asked to pass a similar resolution. It was decided that the letter be referred to the Parliamentary Committee and the Clerk was instructed i ta call an early meeting of such Committee.

MAGISTRATES AND TEETOTAL RELIEVING OFFICFR.

It will be remembered that recently Annie Wakefield, a widow, was summoned by the National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty te Children for neglecting her children, and it was stated that the relieving officer had refused her out-relief. The Chairman of the Bench (Mr. Thomas) said the relieving officer was a teetotaler, and he supposed if be saw anyone take a glass of beer, it would be enough, and he went on to say the woman was entitled to out-door relief. . _

Mr. Cooke now submitted an application to the Board for relief for the woman and two children. The Clerk (Mr. Ashton) said the National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children had bad the case under observation since 1901, and in May, 1901, Messrs. Thomas and Littler sentenced the woman to three months hard labour. In December last she was brought before the same magistrates, and similar evidence was given, and the case was adjourned for a month so that relief could be applied for. The Chairman ("Jr. Baxter) : The question is : such a woman entitled to out-door relief? Mr. Morris : I think Mr. Thomas has gone out of his way to insult our officer. Mr. Wood said the insinuation behind the magistrates remark was most unjust. Mr. Cooke did not allow prejudice, creed, or his temperance principles to inter- fere with his duties. (Hear, hear.) Alderman Norman said some magistrates seemed to think it right to cast slurs upon respectable people in this way. As a magistrate and as a guardian, he pro- tested strongly against what had taken place in court. He moved a resolution approving of Mr. Cooke's action, and protesting against the conduct of the magistrates. Mr. J. H. Davies seconded, and the motion was carried unanimously. The Board declined to allow the woman out-door relief, and decided to take the children into the house.

WIDNES GAS AND WATER SCHEME. COUNCIL'S PROPOSALS REJECTED BY BURGESSES.

The most important meeting in the municipa history of Widnes was held on Tuesday, at noon, in the Town Hall,his Worship the Mayor(Alderman S. Owens) presiding. The meeting was intended to obtain the burgesses' sanction to the new Parliamentary bill now before the Commons, whereby it is proposed to repeal certain sections of the Widnes Improvement Act of 1875. The new Act provides that the profits of the gas and water undertakings shall, after provision has been made for the paying off of mortgages and expenses in the workiug of the same, go to the General District Rate. Fully 500 people attended the meeting, and the work- ing men, landlords, and works' managers gave the Mayor a very trying time. The Town Clerk explained the suggested changes stating that all moneys derived as profits from the gas and water works under the existing Acts were devoted to the provision of a sinking fund for the carrying on of the concerns exclusively. The sum and substance of the new Bill upon which they were asked to vote was whether, after the payment of the necessary expenses the profits should go to the relief of the rates. Mr. Moss I protest against the confiscation of the profits of the gas works on behalf of the landlords. (Loud applause.) The Mayor hoped that all we:e acquainted with the provisions of the Bill. The remainder of the clauses of the Bill, which contained many necessary improvements, he thought would meet with their approval, and he would ask them to vote upon the Bill with the exception of the Gas and Water Profits Clause, which would be discussed later on. All but one of the clauses of the Bill were passed unanimously. - As the meeting was very packed, and many persons were still unable to gain admission, Councillor Welding moved that the meeting be adjourned to be held at a later date in the drill hall (uproar, and cries of" Go on with it now,") to which a workman replied, " We can't afford to lose more time. Why didn't you hold it in the evening ?" Mr. James White said that if the new bill was passed the price of gas would be raised. He would absolutely refuse to allow the council to handle the gas and water profits. Under the existing conditions cheap gas had been of the greatest importance to the ratepayers of the town. Although a property owner, he deprecated any change in the system, and begged that more considera- tion be given to the slot meter gas consumers. Councillor Lewis spoke strongly in favour of the new bill, and asked the burgesses as consumers to ree that their representatives managed the gas and water under- takings in the best possible way. Gas which cost Is. to make cost the ordinayy consumer is. 3d. and the possessor of a slot mhter got 44 feet for H. The consumer of over 3,000,000 feet got it at is. Id. and for motive power at 11d. The average price was only a small fraction over cost. He claimed that the policy of giving manufacturers cheap gas should not be interfered with, but the Council should have discretionary powers to deal with any surplus profits. A large number of burgesses and gas consumers in the Sown wanted something more than cheap gas. To say that the profits would go to the benefit a the land-

ST HELENS.

CAROL SINGERS' RICH HAUL

IA remarkably successful effort has just been made in connection with the new Wesleyan Church which is to ! be built at Denton's Green, St.tielens. In April, 1904, a ' Sunday school, in which divine services have since been held, was opened at Denton's Green. The land and buildings had cost £2,500, and when opened there was a debt of £964 on the school. This has been cleared, and the congregation is taking steps for the erection of a new church. Notice was issued that on New Year's Eve the carol singers connected with the church would go out for the purpose of making a collection, which would be the nucleus of the church building fund. It is now reported that the singers brought back crsh and promises amounting to £262 10s., which is surely a record for any band of carol singers in this district „2

COLLIERY ROOF FALL. f 4211

John M`Gorty, a collier, aged 35, 7, Gertrude-street, St. Helens Junction, met with a shocking death on Monday at the Sutton Heath Colliery. M'Gorty was working in the mine with his father-in-law, Robert Wiseman' and both men were buried by a huge fall of roof, which brought down the timbering at a place where roads of the mine met. The fall was heard by a man named James Lunt, who worked near. After an hour's work Wiseman was dug out, and he was found not to be severely injured. The other man had been buried in the midst of the fall and was quite dead when the rescuers managed to reach him.

PAWNING STOLEN BOOTS.

WARNING TO PAWNBROKERS.

Thomas Ackers, an aged boot-stitcher, of Lancaster, was charged on remand at St. Helens on Saturday with begging and also with stealing a pair of boots value 4s. lid. Police-constable Murrant arrested the man on Friday,27th ult., for begging.He was then found to have pawnticket in his possession, and it was found that Ackers had sent a man from a common lodging-house to pledge the boots. A young man named Platt spoke to the boots being pawned at Mr. Grundy's by an 'elderly man named Robinson. .

The Chairman (Ur. J. B. Leach) : We are surprised at you letting this money go on these boots. We think there is plenty of evidence about them to show they were stolen. _ _

Platt : It is just the class of boots these people buy. In many cases we have new boots offered. The Chairman : \Vell, these have never been used, and his sending the other man to pledge them was a very suspicious business altogether. We think you might have been a little more careful. Chief-inspector Goodall said that prisoner had been sent to gaol for fourteen days by the Liverpool magistrates for begging, and he only came out on the day that he was locked up. He must have come direct to St. Helens.

Ackers was sentenced to six weeks imprisonment. TRAGIC. DEATH OF A ST. HELENS GUARDIAN. Mr. John Rimmer, landlord of the Clarence Hotel Duke-street, and a member of the Prescot Board of Guardians, died somewhat suddenly on Sunday. Mr. Riramer was sixty-four years of age. For over fifty years he had been a respected member of the guild and congregation of Lowe House Church, and in 189 i he was elected to the Board of Guardians. He had not been in very "good health for some time, and during the Ipast week was greatly upset by the death of his sister. On Sunday, soon after half-past twelve, he prepared to go to the funeral, and took his place in the mourning coach to go to the cemetery. Mr. Rimmer was then seized with illness, and he had to be carried back, into house, apparently in a fainting condition. He never properly rallied, and although Dr. Officer was called in death took place at half-past two.

HAYDOCK MINERS WANT A STRIKE.

At a meeting in Manchester on Saturday of the Lancashire and Cheshire 111iners' Federation the result of the ballot taken for dealing with non-unionist miners at the Elaydock collieries of Messrs. Richard Evans and Co., was declared as follows :—Queen, Princess, and Leigh pits, 812 in favour of giving notices to stop work and 29 against; Golborne, 298 in favour, against 36 ; Wood Pit, 169 in favour, 10 against ; and Old Boston, 372 in favour and 21 against.—Before the extreme course indicated in these figures is taken a deputation of the General Committee will propably wait on thr employers.

NEWTON URBAN COUNCIL.

he monthly meeting of the Newton Urban Council was held at the Earlestown Town Hall on Monday even- ing. Mr. T. O'rmand presided, and there were also present :—Dr. J. N. Patterson, and Messrs. W. Thompson, W. Collingwood, L. E. Pilkington, A. Tunstall, T. Ritchie. M. Setoo, A. Smith, G. F. Clarke, J. Naylor, F. Harwood, E. T. Evans, C. B. F. Borron, and C. Cole (clerk), A. TRIAL OF GAS -CONTROLLERS. At a meeting of the Gas Committee, on December 16th, the Gas Engineer reported that a trial of several of " Gunning's controllers" had been made in certain parts of the township, which trial had proved satis- factory ; and that if this system were adopted and the controllers purchased, there would be, he estimated, a net saving per year in labour and gas of about .£llO. It was resolved that about 180 controllers be purchased (out of the reserve fund) and fixed on the street lamps in two of the lighting sections which include outlying districts, as a test before the system is adopted for the whole of the street lamps in the district. The minutes were approved. REPAIR OF ROADS.

At a meeting of the Highways Committee on December 16th, the Surveyor submitted his estimates of the probable expenditure on main roads in respect of the year 1908-9. It was resolved that the same be approved, and that the Council be recommended to apply to the Lancashire County Council for a sum of £Bl2 10s. for ordinary repair and maintenance, .£32.6 13s. Id. for the repairing of the carriage-way of High-street from Park-road to the old Stocks, and £230 for the flagging of the foot- path on the north side of St. Helens-road, from Market street to Swan-road. The minutes were adopted.

NOTIFICATION OF BIRTHS ACT.

At a meeting of the Sanitary Committee a letter was read from the Medical Officer of Health enclosing letter which he had received from the County Medical Officer of Health relative to the adoption of the Notifi- cation of Births Act, 1907, and in which he (Or. Sergeant) stated that he was strongly of opinion that all local authorities should adopt such Act, for it was the only way by which they could become acquainted with the information as to the surroundings of infants which was so important for statistical and precautionary measures.

The Clerk called attention to the circular letter issued by the Local Government Board on the subject, in which they stated that in their opinion there was no occasion for imposing upon parents and others the obligation of notifying births unless steps were taken to carry out the ultimate object of the measure, viz. : The giving of advice and instruction to those who had charge of the infants, and that in ordinary circum- stances they would not, be prepared to consent to tbe adoption of the Act unless it appeared that arrange- ments had been made for that purpose. It was resolved that the Council be recommended to take the necessary steps for the adoption of the Act. The minutes were agreed to.

INSPECTOR OF NCISANCES. On the proposition of the Chairman, it was resolved that Mr. Henry Ashcroft be re-appointed (subject to the sanction of the Local Government Board) inspector of nuisances for the district, for one year from the 24th January instant, with a salary at the rate of £l5O per annum for his services ; and that a moiety of such salary be claimed out of the county fund.

NOTICE OF MOTION. Mr. A. Smith (chairman of the sanitary committee) gave notice :—• That at a special meeting of the Council to be held on Honday, the 17th February next, he win move the adoption by the Council of the Notification of Births Act, 1907."

ROYAL COURT THEATRE. "Lucky Durham." Another of Wilson Barrett's popular plays " Lucky Durham "has been the attraction at the Royal Court Theatre this week, and though this is the play's third appearance here it has attracted capital audiences. Messrs. Armitage and Leigh's company is a very strong one, and only pleasant things can be said of the way in which the play under notice is performed. The story is not very elaborately woven, but it has novel features and lends itself well to dramatic treat, ment. John Durham, an American millienaire, comes "home "and enters Society—the Society led by Lady Mountfallon and her husband. Great efforts are made to capture this eligible parti for the daughter of the noble house of Mountfallon, but the millionaire only smiles a sad sardonic smile. Early on the secret is out. John Durham is Lord Mountfallon's illegitimate son. He has fought his way to success, tended lovingly his deeply-wronged mother, and come over to England with revenge in his heart. The Mount- fallons are numbered among the impoverished nobility, and the family estate coming into the market, John Durham quietly buys it "lock, stock, and barrel" and arrives on the scene in the midst of a severe " family jar " caused by the news that Mountfallon Castle has passed from the hands of his Lordship, and they are liable to be summarily turned out. It was John Durham's intention to thus secure and enjoy, together with his mother, the inheritance of which he had been deprived by the baseness of an erring father, but in a touching closing scene the i nobleness of Durham's character s exemplified by an act of great magnanimity. He restores the estates to their lineal owner, and departs with a mind at ease and a heart contented by the love of Helen Vallerton, I the heroine of the play. There is a capital scene in , Act 2 when " Lucky " Durham finds the " bar sinister " a bar in reality to his love-suit. Helen's father has strict n-)tions and a deep concern for Society with a capital S. Lord Mountfallon desires the fair Helen for his son, the Hon. Reginald Steighton' and his opposition is the last straw. Durham boldly declares his birth and identity to the two men, and defies his father just at the moment Helen enters the room. Tableau ! Mr. Edmund Kennedy gives a very polished and effective impersonation of the title-role but if any- thing his restraint is too marked, and his delibera- tion too pronounced. He is, unquestionably, an accomplished actor and makes John Durham a real and engaging personality. As Helen Vallerton, Miss Alice Esther Belmare is charming. She has a pretty part and is moreover endowed with good looks and lady-like graces to an extent that makes the role a perfectly natural one. Mr. Arthur Leigh has quite a happy time as the empty-headed Hon. Reggie. His humour is of the spontaneous order, and he performs in lighter vein with ease and cleverness. The parts of Lady. Mountfallon and her daughter, the Hon. Margaret are also well taken by Miss Rosabel Dodd and Miss Blanchette Grand, the former being con- spicuously good in the hysterical scene with his Lordship near the end. Miss Louisa Gourlay makes a very tenderhearted and sympathetic Mrs. Durham,and there is distinct promise in Mr. A. E. Walker's acting as Mr. Vallerton. Lord Mountfallon is played with dignity and force by Mr. T. R. Lockwood. The minor , parts are all in very capable hands Mr. J. A. Gerald , and Mr. John Hartt both making very notable litte " hits."

MISS 'BESSIE RIGNOLD AS " LEAH KLESCLINA." NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMIIE.

I For the third time in Warringeon, but for the first time in the new theatre, " Leah Kieschna," Charles Frohinan's greatest dramatic success,will be presented I for one week commencing Monday next. The trend of public taste as regards the drama is very interest- ing. Not so long ago it would have been almost impossible to have found audiences sufficient to keep a play of the aim and character of "Leah Kleschna on the road -but now, all that is changed, and the great moral drama goes on its way, prerching its lesson of mercy, and vieing with the Church in its usefulness. The story is well known, but will bear reiteration. Paul Sylvaine is an idealist, a dreamer, and he has views on one's duty towards the evildoers lof this world. He has an infinite pity for the poor thiefand holds that in nine cases out of tea he has known no better. He says effect that there is as much good in the thief as there is in any one of us, only it is deeper down, and has never had a chance against the circum- stances of life and environment. When he finds " Leah Kleschna " in his room, come there to steal the famous jewels, he does not do as others do—hand her over to the police, but he talks to her, argues with her, and put her position clearly before her. She ultimately breaks down, and returns to her father with intent to do good in the future. The outcome of this resolution is the wonderful fourth act, whose like has seldom if ever been before an audience. The same splendid company that played with such effect on the last occasion is still in requisition. Mr. William F. Grant who plays " Paul Svlvaine " was brought over to this country by Mr. Wilson Barrett, who was the first actor to recognise his great talent. Mr. William Clayton who plays " Kleschna " is acknowledged to be one of the most notable actors on the- English stage. There is no part in contemporary or Shakesperean plays that he has not essayed. Miss Bessie Riguold, who plays " Leah " is recognised as 003 of the best dramatic artistes of the day. She is the daughter of William Riunold, and commenced her stage career as a child. She played leads in all her uncle's (George Rignold) Shakesperean productions in Australia and round the world, and has since played practically every leading part in he legitimate drama in this country. The whole of the productioa straight from London is travelled complete.

Two Candidates in the Field There will be a contest for the representation of St. Austin's Ward rendered vacant by the elevation of Councillor Burton to an Alderrnanic seat. The election will take place on Monday week, the 20th inst. Yester- day was the last day for the receipt of nominations and papers were handed in on behalf of Mr. John Maginn (I) and Mr. James Broadhurst (C)• Mr. John Magina, R tddon Court, Ktmtsford-road, furniture dealer, is proposed by David Tinnion,seconded by James Weaver, and assented to by Percy Mather, Thomas Daly, Thomas B rchall Carter, James Carter, William Joseph Wilcock, Arthur Browne, Thomas Crarnpton and George Weaver. In a second paper Mr. Maginn is proposed by William Crosskill, seconded by Joseph Elwell, jun., and assented to by James Oliver, Martin Jordan, Thomas Kelly, James Jay, Michael Jordan, Richard Welsby, John Holford Pepper, and Seth Hunt. THE VOICE OF LABOUR. Edited by John Workman. .u.s.s. must be addressed to John Workman, " Examiner " Office, Warrington and should be sent in not later than Wednesday in each week. Readers of this column are invited to assist in making it interesting and helpful to social workers by contributing themselves. Correspondents must write on one side of the paper only, and endeavour to make their letters and notes short and to the point. A nom-de-plume may be used, but the real name of the writer should be forwarded in confidence to the Editor of the column.

-- THE TRADES COUNCIL AND ITS CRITICS. Sir, —Allow me to convey, through your column, my compliments to Messrs. F. Stott and T. Plumb. I desire these gentlemen to know that, after a short family consultation on the question, 1 have reluctantly decided not "to disclose my identity " and must therefore decline with thanks T.P.'s serio-comic invitation to "Come from behind that non-de-plume ?" —The suspicions of the ex-secretary of the Trades Council (F.S.) are aroused and he is a little afraid that " Onlooker " " Spectator " and " Trade Unionist " represent one individual (probably " not " a Trade Unionist) who may " NOT POSSESS" a "MORAL RIGPIT" (large capitals please) to criticise Councillor F. Stott, J.P., the, great Mr. Plumb, and the Trades Council generally. My word, John, just fancy that ! Why did you not present our cards to the immaculate 1' S. and the mighty T.P. and thus secure a judicial decision as to our locus standi in Trades Union circles are inserting our "scurrilous " contributions in your valuable column ?

Just to clear the air, Sir, allow me to blow my ot4n trumpet a wee bit. I am a Trades Unionist, and (although never a member of the Warrington Trades Council) have given several years useful service to the Trades Union movement. I have no business connec- tion with either " Spectator " or " Trades Unionist" but am "running on my own." I have written (previous to this) two contributions in your column, which have (I am pleased to say), proved very effective for they have directly resulted in Councillor Stott moving hi i 4 resolution re "Municipal Coal Supply " whilst another member of the Trades Council, Mr. T. Plumb, has " lost his head." " Sufficient unto the day are the fruits thereof."

"ONLOOKER."

Sir,—l have noticed in the Voice of Labour for the last few weeks a controversy between several cor- respondents including " Trade Unionist," F. Stott and T. Plumb, relating to the Trades Council.

I do not intend to add feel to the fire, but to simply point out further proof of the contention for an Actionist Labour Party, advocated by " Currentis " some weeks ago. I have it from good authority that Messrs. F. Stott, T. Plumb and other members of the Trades Council are Socialists. That being so, one cannot wonder at the inactivity displayed ! ! In addition to the above fact, add the brotherly! feeling exhibited by Mr. T. Plumb, in last week's issue, where he says "If Onlooker ' dare not dis- close his identity I do not hesitate to call him a coward etc."

The language is typical of Socialists and only goes to show what we will get if these fellow-creatures get the upper hand.

SUUM CUIQUE."

Sir, -In the " Exsininer " of the 21st ult., I see " Onlooker " was devoted to his self-appointed task of criticising the Trades Council with a view to its reformation. I think the hopelessness of this employ- ment has already begun to dawn on him, for his letter with its questionable taste, and bad tone throughout, already proclaims the " mind distressed." He refers to my letter of the previous week, incidentally, but neither " Onkoker " nor " Trade Unionist " grasps the most salient argument I put forward, or if they do they ignore its significance, and hence go woefully astray. The" fact " I pointed out was, the Warring- ton Trades Unions do not want political independence. On the contrary, they are satisfied with the orthodox Liberal end Tory parties, and therefore, with the present Trades Council. How does " Onlooker" and " Trade Unionist " expect to redress social wrongs and initiate social reforms, except by independent political action on the part of the workers ? To blame the Trades Council for not doing what it has no power to do, is like blaming a wheelbarrow for not going faster instead of blaming the man who holds the handles. " Onlooker " is starting at the wrong end ; it is the rank and file of the Trade Unions he should ful- minate against, instead of the poor long suffering Trades Council delegates. They have done all their Unions delegated or instructed them to do. I admit the importance of Housing, Education, and kindred questions, and would like to see them solved. But " Onlooker "in his eagerness, fights as ono " beating the air." Let me beg of him to call a halt in this crusade, and do a little missionary work among his fellow workers, if he is a unionist, at his branch meet- ings. If I understand the Trades Council rightly, it is the outward and visible' sign, of an inward and spiritual belief that however strong a union might be, there was something lacking ; that "something " was the co-operation of other unions. The idea is a good one, and well suited the old Trade Union notion of a general strike but 19 a general strike is impossible, the primal necessity of Trades Councils seems to be gone. I believe there is greater necessity than ever,for combined action on the part of the workers. but it must be for political warfare not strikes. The Trades Unionist battles of the future will be fought on the floor of the House of Commons. Trades Unions have a political machine now, if they will only u.e it, far more efficient than the Trades Councils. If " Onlooker" and others who think like him, will help to resucitate the Labour Party (the old L.R.C.) in Warrington they will be doing more to bring about reforms which they desire than by thundering at the local Trades Council. If the trades unions of Warrington believed in political independeace, John. Iwe Warrington workers would have a " Voice of Labour " in St Stephens next election. . . .

Regarding the co'ntributions of Messrs. Stott and Plumb, the first-named has evidently not much faith in your editorship John, if he thinks you would allow one man to fill a column with letters under different names. I would gently remind him to) of the proxerb about " glass houses." Respecting Mr. Plumb's letter, I am driven to admit that if it is representative of the Council, " Onloakees " description of that body is the correct one, and as I don't want kicking, I still sign myself, SPECTATOR.

,

PADGATiI WESLEYAN SCHOOL,

IR

===== 0002601_19080111_art0062.txt | included=true | included =====
Annual Tea Party.

The annual tea party in connection with Padgate Wesleyan School was held in the schoolroom on Thurs- day evening. Rev. W. Mellor presided. There was a very large attendance. Mr. F. Ward in reading the secretary's report said they relied entirely upon the public for support and without that support they could not got on successfully Owing to the great increase in the number of scholars they were anticipating building a new school as there was not sufficient accommodation in the present schoolroom. He hoped they •would do all in their power to assist them in the support of the new school. They had 216 scholars on the books-112 males and 134 females an increase or eight during the year. The average attendance was : Mornings 78, after- noons 150, which was almost the same as last year. The Bible class had a membership of $O, and the average attendance was 17 but there is room for more. During the year £177 17s. Sd. was contributed to the Savings Bank by 60 members. They has sustained a great loss by the death of Mr, H. Ward, one of their superintendents, who for over twenty-five years had rendered untiring and faithful service to the school. A memorial tablet had been placed in the Church by the scholars and members of the congregation as a token of esteem and regard for his past services to the Church and school generally. Mr. Upson emphasized the value of skilled teachers in Sunday schools as well as in day schools. He also paid a high tribute to Vie work Mr. Ward had done for them.

The Rev. W. Mellor also spoke. During the evening the following programme was gone through :—Glees, " Eldorada " and " Good-night" by the choir, Dr. Bennett conducting ; recitation by one of the scholars ; songs by Miss Woodland ;viola solos, Master Millington; amusing dialogues, "Washing thy" (in costume), "Women's Rights," "Black Bachelor " ; musical drills with parasols and gipsy ance in costume.

THE WORKHOUSE SCANDAL.

DR. FLITCROFT APPLIES FOR A TESTIMONIAL.

The Board and .‘ Christian Charity."

L.G.B. INSPECTOR'S STRICTURES ON "TITTLE-TATTLE."

There was another echo of the Workhouse Scandal a the Warrington Board of Guardians meeting on Wednesday and some severe strictures on the Board's methods were passed by Mr. Lowry, Local Government Board inspector, who paid one of his periodical visits. Mr. W. Boughton presided over a good attendance. The CLERK reported that he bad received a letter from Dr. Flitcroft in which he said :—" I notice that you have advertised for a successor, and I now find it would be convenient to me if the Board will so arrange that I may vacate my office on December 31st, 1907. I shall be pleased if the Board in respect to the manner in which I have discharged my duties as Workhouse Medical Officer will grant me a testimonial."—(The effect of the letter is to reduce the period of notice from three months to one month.)

===== 0002601_19080111_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
A TRIBUTE TO THE Docroa.

Mr. BILLINGE moved that the Board grant Dr. Plitcroft a testimonial as to the manner in which he bad performed his duties during the fourteen months he had been with them. There might he little differences of opinion on the point, but he thought they were all convinced that the doctor had done his work well and that what he had accomplished reflected great credit upon him. Mr. SwroN seconded. A PROTEST AND A RETORT. Miss BRO (DBENT : After what has °enured I protest very strongly against the granting of a testimonial, and I 1 hope my protest will be recorded. _

- . The CFIAIRMAN : Do you move an amendment ? Miss BROADBENT : Yes. Mr. FURNESS : lam sorry to see some slight opposi- tion is to be offered to a charitable recommendation of this character. It is almost a reflection on the Christian charity of the Board—(hear, hear)—that opposition should be brought against a man having a character. i We know full well as a Board that Dr. Flitcroft hag done a great deal for the institution, not only as far as , the patients are concerned, but from an economical ; standpoint. He has saved the institution a considerable sum, and I do think it is a reflection on the Board that we should discuss the amendment which has been pro- posed by Miss Broadbent. It almost reminds one of , the old days of the Vendetta,when a man was pursued by ! the Spaniard until his life was taken from him. I hope. , the amendment Miss Broadbent has so ungenerously I proposed will be defeated. The CITAIRMRN : It is a question of a negative vote. Mr. JOLLEY : After the remarks of Mr. Furness it would be a disgrace to the Board if no further com- ments were offered. Mr. Furness has made some rather impassioned observations about Christian charity and some very unworthy reflections on Miss Broadbent. I venture to think that. at any rate, without discussing the question of whether we call ourselves Christians or not, as civilized Englishmen in the.twentieth century we must regard it as a primary duty to speak the truth. Dr. Flitoroft's request is that a testimonial should be granted to him in respect of the manner in which be has discharged his duties. If that testimonial is granted, I presume it will be written by the Clerk and will state the truth. If it states the ttuth the testimonial will not be of any benefit to the doctor but a positive hindrance to him getting employment at any future time. If Mr. Furness suggests that the Board as a public body is to grant a false testimonial he is at liberty to suagest it,but I venture to think the majority of the Board will not concur in that line of action. strongly endorse the brief protest Miss Broadbent has entered. It is absolutely inconsistent with the honour and integrity of a public body to grant a false testi- monial and a trite testimonial would be no use at all. If Dr. Flitcroft had asked for a testimonial merely relating to the actual medical work, that would have been another matter altogether. I have no fault to find. I have frequently bornetestiinony to the admirable manner in which that has been discharged, but that is not what be asks for. _ _

The CLERK : I certainly should confine the testi- monial to that, and not enter upon anything else. Mr. JOLLEY : His duties as resident medical officer I include relations with certain other °Facials, with the nurses and the patients. It is not a question merely of purely professional work. The CLERK : I should have thought so personally. Mr. JOLLY : I think it would be nothing short of a public scandal that a body whose reputation has already been besmirched should allow a resolution of this kind to pass without protest.

===== 0002601_19080111_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
MORE DISCUSSION:

The Rev. T. R. Maw Haws said that after the high encomiums which had been passed on Dr. Flitcroft's work, he did not see what else the Board could do but pr tut the testimonial. They were not justified in dragging into the question something else which had gone b.f. The doctor asked simply for honest and fair treatment and he had been very highly spoken of by members of the Board. The Board was in duty bound to grant the doctor what he asked for. Dis resignation at a month's notice was a voluntary act and he had obliged them thus far. It was not right to bring in the foreign element in determining the question. Mr. JAMES EVANS said the action against Dr. Flitcroft had been settled outside the Board room and be thought the Board should now leave that matteralone. Ile had never heard any complaint about Dr. Flitcrof t's treatment of the patients as a medical roan. The Board should disabuse their minds of the other question and grant a testimonial on the lines suggested by the Clerk.

Mr. R. FOR:SHAW said the office of resident medical officer covered more duties than medical atfendsnce on the patients and he maintained that Dr. Plitcroft had not discharged all the duties to the Board's satisfaction. Would any member of the Board in his private capacity grant a testimonial to a man who had served him as Dr. Macron had served them ? He (Mr. Forshaw) did not say a word against him as a doctor. Rev. T. R. MATTIIF.ws : He only asks for a testimonial to his work as medical officer. _

Mr. AlATintrt said he could not support the resolution although he did not want to say anything harsh about the doctor.,

Ihe CHAIRMAN said he was certain the Clerk might be depended upon to give a testimonial which would be valuable to Dr. Flitcrott without compromising the Mord in the least.

The CLERK said no man spoke more strongly and yet in a more neutral way than Mr. A. Brooks. If the Board instructed him (the Clerk) to give a testimonial be thought he could not do better than follow the words of Mr. Brooks as to the manner in which Dr. Flitcroft had discharged his duties. Mr. A. BROOKS said he had no objection to that, but he would certainly ask thst the context of the speech should be given. Although the Board might be taunted with having no Christianity charity, they must bear in mind that their testimonials ought to be of some value. It would have been far more to the doctor's advantage if he htd left without asking for a testimonial. It was no credit to the Board that certain matters were settled outside the Board room : they ought to have been settled inside. He acknowledged a deep debt of gratitude to Dr. Flitcroft for the work he did up to July, but to grant him a testimonial after what had occurred since then would he to make their testimonials practically value- less.

===== 0002601_19080111_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
TEST MONTAL GRANTED.

On a role being taken the Board decided to grant tbe testimonial, by 15 votes to 7. Tho CLERK It is quite rindrstood that I should pnrely limit myself to the discharge of his duties as doctor snd not criter open any question between the doctors and nurses, the slander case, or anything else. (Hear, hear.)

THE QUESTION OF A SUCCESSOR. NEW SYSTENI DECIDED UPON.

At a meeting of the House Committee on December 29th, it was resolved that a resident medical officer be advertised for at a salary of £l5O per annum with the usual ollowances ; also that the Board be recommended to advertise for a deputy medical officer at a salary of £5O per annum. At another meeting the medical officer's report bock aimed by T. E. Flitcroft, under date December 20rd, 1907, was read, and contained some adverse comments on the training of probationers as hitherto conducted. These comments being considered unjustifiable, the committee recorded their strong disapproval of the doctor's action in the matter. . .

I --A—t-ern'p"o-r.a—ry arrangement made with Dr. Hodgkinson to perform the whole of the duties of medical (Miner at a remuneration of half a guinea per day was approved nntil the vacant appointments are filled. The CLRRIC said he had advertised for a resident medical officer and had received 12 applications, one of which had been withdrawn. Mr. BILLINGE remarked that since the meeting of December 20th, when there was a divided vote on the question of whether they should have a resident medical officer or not, a change had come over the minds of the Guardians that it would be advisable at I this inneture to have a non-resident medical officer. 1 After some discussion on procedure, Mr. MlOOlOll formally moved and Mr. FURNESS seconded the adop- tion of the House Committee's minutes. Mr. JAMES EVANS moved an amendment that the order of the resolution regarding the new appointments be reversed, and that advertisements be issued for a non-resident medical officer and for a deputy medical officer who should reside in the institution, the salaries to be £lOO each. Mr. NIATHER seconded.

......—...—.. Dr. PATTERSON said it was his idea when the matter of appointing Dr. Flitcroft was first discussed, to continue the employment of Dr. Braide and have a deputy medical officer resident in the institution. He supported Councillor Evans' proposal, but suggested

that the salaries should be £BO for the non-residen officer and £l2O for the deputy and resident officer; the latter to give an undertaking not to practise in War- rington for five years. Mr. FURNESS said that on Mr. Jolley's admission Dr. Flitcroft had saved the Guardians between £4OO and £5OO, so that on economical grounds alone the Guar- dians would not be doing their duty to the ratepayers if they did not have a resident medical officer. LOCAL GOVERNMENT BOARD INSPECTOR'S ADVICE.

Mr. Lowxv, Local Government Board inspector, was asked to give his views on the question. He said he could only think of one other Union where there was merely a single resident officer. So far as he knew, everything was going on well there, but roost Unions with which he was acquainted, had either got a visiting medical officer only, a visiting medical officer with one or more resident assistants, or a resident medical officer in chief charge with assistants. He was bound to say that he very much favoured the practice of haying the chief medical officer visiting and non-resident, as it was advantageous in many ways. The doctor brought to his work a certain freshness and a lack of that institu- tionalism which was apt to fall upon any man who spent his whole time in the institution. Coming into contact with outside practice was a good thing for a medical man as it kept him more wide awake and it was therefore better for the patients. It was almost inevitable when they advertised for a resident medical officer at a salary which was not very high that th9y would get a young man. A great many men were old before their years and were perfeotly capable of doing what might be required of them quite properly, but prima facie a young man WES at a dis- advantage. He did not carry the weight be should with the Guardians and officers and with the nurses whom he was supposed to control. It would be a great advantage to have as non-resident officer, a man of standing in the town. Of course, the bulk of the work must fall on the resident officer. The Inspector con- cluded by expressing the hope that everything would go well and that the new resident officer would have perhaps a happier time than the old one. Mr. JOLLEY said that as far as the actual merit of either principle was concerned he believed there wag nothing to choose. If they got a really competent and suitable man they would obtain splendid results by either method. He would not vote either way. Mr. Gill also abstained from voting. ! The Board decided by a good majority to adopt the new system of a chief medical officer who should be non-resident, with a resident deputy-medical officer. The salaries were flied at £lOO each. The other minutes were approved. THE INSPECTOR'S STRICTURES. "TITTLE-TATTLE AND EVIL-SPEAKING."

At the close of the meeting the Chairman invited Mr. Lowry to address the Board, jocularly remarking that they liked to be flattered. Mr. LOWRY said he wished the Board a happy New Year—a happier one than the last one had been. It would be mere affectation to stand there and say noth- ing about the unfortunate occurrences which had taken place, and he hoped they would be more fortunate in their medical arrangements in the future. He might say from his own knowledge of things—and he hoped I they would not take it amiss—that in the Warrington Union there was perhaps more tittle-tattle (Mr. Mather: That is true)and evil speaking,he was very sorry to have to say, than in any other Union in his district. It was a matter which he regarded with great pain, and ; he hoped they would feel he was speaking as though he I were himself a member of the Warrington Board of Guardians. He tried to identify himself as much as be I could with every Board with which he came into contact I and he took a pride in the honour of every Board in which he was concerned. He hoped and prayed they were not to have any scandals in his district such as bad appeared in other parts of the kingdom. Therefore, he said these words—which they might regard as offensive—because although they had not started on the downward path, he could not help thinking their faces were turned rather in that direction. He was not speaking like that without very fair reason. He had experienced in his own person the extreme readiness there was on the part of some members of the Board, at all events, to give heed to scandal uttered by quite irresponsible persons and to accept statements as true without any evidence at all I behind them. If that sort of thing was going to take place on the part of many members, they were bound to come to a bad scandal eventually, and he hoped they would avoid that. The way to avoid it was to act with more corporate feeling. It was impossible for one who had observed the recent proceedings not to see that there had been a great deal of partizanship on one side or the other on the Board, and he thought they would be wise to avoid individual action on the part of I Guardians. They must remember that an individual Guardian was no-one, but that all action must be done by committees or by the Guardians as a body. It was a very valuable privilege that members of the Board possessed of visiting the Workhouse. It was only one of recent years which had been granted to individual Guardians and it might I very easily be abused. He thought individual Guardians should remember when they visited the Workhouse that they were not there to listen to any gossip that might be heard but to see for themselves 1 how things were going on. He must apologise for having preached a sermon and for having refrained from any flattery but he thought it was the part of a friend to refrain from it and be hoped they would not feel hurt at anything he had said. He had not the least doubt that every member of the Board was most anxious that the whole of the adminis- tration should be pure and economical and proper in every way. All that was necessary in order that those results might ensue should be that they should all work together as one body and not impute motives to one another, hut to admit that other people might be honest as well as themselves. A PROTEST.

Mr. FORSTIAW : I resented the remarks of the Judge in this case. and I shall dissociate myself from any tittle-tattle. I say this Board is not guilty of the charge. The CHAIRMAN : There may be differences of opinion about that. I would suggest that if the Chiar- man of the House Comm itteo wants to put any specific question he should do so. Mr. FORSHAW : I shall give my position up if lam going to be covered with a charge like this. I resent it. The CHAIRMAN: You are only one of twenty-eight of us.

Mr. FORSRAW : I am not that one, any way. (Laughter.) The Rev. T. R. MATTHEWS : We are very much obliged to the Inspector for the words of wisdom he has spoken to us. The meeting then closed.

ADULT SCHOOL.

===== 0002601_19080111_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
Interesting Experiment at Warrington.

THE MAYOR AND INFANTILE MORTALITY.

To its numerous institutions Warrington has added an adult school. It opened on Sunday morning at 8.45 at the British Workmen's Coffee House, Borsemarket- street, and is an attempt to solve to some extent the everlasting problem of " How to reach the masses." The school will meet every week at the above time and it is intended to be a genuine brotherhood. It will be unsectarian and democratic, every man having a voice in the management. Well-organized adult schools consist of gatherings for Bible study with educational classes, lecturettes on scientific, hygienic and other topics, savings banks, libraries, coal clubs, social and recreative clubs, &c. Nearly 500 new schools have been opened in England in three years an there is every indication that the Warrington enterprise will be taken up with enthusiasm. The inaugural meeting, was held in the Kendrick- street schools on Saturday evening the Mayor (Alder- man Smethurst) presiding over a good attendance. Amongst those present were :—The Misses Smethurst, the Revs.W. Eracecamp and C.Hary y-Cook, Councillor Ball, Dr. C. R. Brown, Mr. and Mrs. C.Jackson Holmes, and Mr. J. A. Ransorne.

The MAYOR pointed out that the school was particu- larly intended for working people and if it did some- thing to remedy the evils which prevailed in our midst it would deserve God's blessing. In the Town Council recently there was a discussion on infantile mortality and his friend the Rev. Sidney Pitt had also made some remarks on the question. He (the Mayor) had often felt it would be a good thing if they could accomplish the object the rev, gentleman had in view—the establishment of a ladies' committee to visit the slum districts and advise the parents as to the feeding and general treatment of their children. He suggested that if such an orgtnization could be formed in connection with the adult school it would be a great help. In Huddersfield and Halifax similar effoas bad been pro- moted with great success, the infantile death rate having been considerably reduced. He trusted the adult school woo It fulfil its objects and bring comfort and happiness to many homes. (Applause.) The Rev. W. Bit ACECAMP related his experiences of an adult school for med in connection with the Parish Church at Sheffield. It met every Sunday morning at 8 o'clock for fifteen years. It began with eight mem- bers, and when he left there were 400. What could be done in Sheffield could be done in any town. (Applause.) It might he said that there was no need for such a school in Warrington. but he contended that there was, because in spite of our free education and the advances we had made, there were plenty of men who could not sign their names or could not write properly. At Sheffield they used to give writing lessons for an hour, and then divide the school into classes for the purpose of Bible study. If they had such an organization in Warrington to teach men general knowledge it would do a valuable and important work (Applause.) Mr. H. RUSSELL BRAYSHAW, one of the secretaries of the Manchester District Adult School Union, said it would rest with the school to play an important part in the civic life of the town. The last seven Lord Mayols of Birmingham had all been members of the adult school and he confidently hoped for the time when the people would apply the same rule to their Members of Parliament. (Laughter.) The movement was making tremendous strides, for the adult schools had now well over 100.000 members. During the past year they had been adding to their membership at the rate of 1,000 per month. Mr. Brayshaw emphasised the need which , existed for the truest and broadest education. Drunken- ness, idleness, lack of thought, ignorance in domestic management were only too apparent for whilst we had progressed in the things in which the State helped the individli,l we had not made corresponding progress in those things in which the individual helped himself. Another nerd of the times was true fellowship. There was room for fellowship between people of all denominations and creeds and the adult school aimed at fostering that spirit. The school would be a co-opera- tive religious society. (Applause.) The Rev. C. HARVEY COOK said he was glad that demphasia was 'hid on religion in connection with the school. It was a good thing that they could have movements in which all could participate and fraternise for the good of each and all. Everything which was uplifting for a man or woman to do was religious, because it went to broaden and widen the human vision, to help them to grasp something of the majesty and wonderful beauty of the world. While the school would have its time allotted to Bible study it would also undertake the study of social questions and science. The sneaker also laid stress on the inculcation of thrift and commended the introduction of home safes by the Warrington Savings Bank. In answer to Councillor Ball, the Rev. W. BRACE. CAMS' said that at first the school would be for men only and it would close in time forattendance at morn- ing service at the chunihes. In other towns there were schools for men and women. . . . .

Dnring the evening songs were very pleasingly given by Miss Plinston. Mr. Norman Moss skilfully played violin solos and Mrs. C. Jackson Holmes delighted the audience with pianoforte solos.

MY DEAR As I write I cannot ,‘elp thinking ot puor little children you have helped during the last few weeks with nice warm clothing. Next week I hope to publish the names of the winners of the prizes I promised. I feel this Competition has done so much good that I shall commence a fresh one for January and February, and shall offer prizes for the best articles received during these months. In the recent Competition several prize-winners emitted to give their full address. Will any of my members who have not received their Chocolates kindly send me a line, and I will at once forward the waiting boxes?

With lots of love, UNCLE NED.

CHRISTMAS STORY.

Dear Uncle Ned,—l am one of your new mem- bers. I like reading the "Children's CorDer " very much. I am writing a little story for Christmas. It is called LITTLE MAY.

"Mamma," eaid May, "it is Christmas Eve to-night; will Santa Claus come?" "Yes," said her mother, "you had better write down what you want." May got a piece of paper and v9rote, "I want a large doll and mail-cart, some bricks to build castles with, a necklace, a story book, and anything else you like to bring me." She handed it to her mother, who read it, and then threw it up• the chimney. Her mother got her some supper and took her to bed. "I don't think I shall go to sleep until I have seen Santa Claus," May said, as she got into bed. But she was very tired that night, and was soon asleep. About midnight Santa Claus came. He thought he would have a joke, so 11-?, put twopence in May's stocking; the other things he tied up into a package and put them at the foot of the bed, on the floor. When May awoke next morning she shouted, "A Merry Christmas!" and jumped out cf bed to see what was in her stocking. But, cnly finding two- pence, she began to cry, and said she would not ask Santa Claus for anything else. But then she spied the package, took it, and jumped into bed, shouting, "Hurray, hurray, hurray!"

's CHRIST _ CHOICE. Winnie lived in a caravan, and slept in the funniest bed ever you saw. When Winnie had gcne to bed one night she lay thinking about her mother, who had died a year ago.lt dsh was Christmas, and Winnie knew what Christmas meant, for she had not always lived in a caravan.

Winme's father often got drunk and beat her and her little brother Alfie, and she was deter- mined to run away. But whom could she run to? She did not know of a relative in the wide world.

As she lay thinking these things over there came a knock at the door of the caravan. Winnie sat up in bed, feeling very frightened. She heard a silvery voice outside saying, "Do not be afraid, Winnie, but open the door." Winnie jumped up and opened the door. What do you think stood before her? A shining angel. The angel said, "It is Christmas, and know you would like a present. You can only have one thing, so think well before you make your choice."

Winnie first thought she would ask for a nice new dress; then she thought she would ask for a lovely new hat; then she thought she would ask for a great big doll. But something said to her, "It would be selfish to ask for any of those things," and then she thought she would ask for a big wooden horse for her little brother, Alfie.

" Be quick, Winnie," said the angel, have many other errands to go." Then the thought came to Winnie, "Ask that your father may give up the nasty drink." Yes, thought Winnie, that would be best cf all, so she said, "Please, kind angel, I would like father to give up the nasty drink." 'N "You have chosen well," said the angel, "and it shall be as you wish." In the morning Winnie thought she would tell her father what the angel said. As she told him he looked very thoughtful. But when she bad told him he said he would give up the think. Winnie threw her arms round his neck and kissed him.

"for I

" But you shall have those other things you wished for. I will buy them with my money instead of buying drink." He kept his promise, and took Winnie and Alfie to live in a nice house.
