-4- A SIMPLE REMEDY.

Said to Relieve Rheumatism, and Anyone Can Prepare it.

Thousands of men and women who have felt the sting and torture of this dread disease which is no respector of age, person, sex, nation or rank, will be interested to know that while for many years rheumatism was con- sidered an incurable disease, now it is one of the easiest afflictions of the human body to conquer. Medical science has proved that it is not a disease in itself, but is a symptom caused hy inactive kidneys. Rheumatism is uric acid in the blood and other waste products of the system, which should be filtered and strained out in the form of urine. The fundt,i.on of the kidneys is to sift these poisons and acids out, and keep the blood clean and pure. The kidneys, however, are of sponge- like substance, the holes or pores of whioh will some- times, either from overwork, cold or exposure, become clogged and inactive, and failing in their function of eliminating these poisons from the blood, they remain iii the veins, decompose, and settling about the joints I and muscles cause the untold pain and suffering of rheumatism, and backache, often producing complies; tions of bladder and urinary disease, weakness, etc. 1 The following simple proscription is said to relieve i the worst cases of rheumatism, because of its direct I action open the blood and kidneys, relieving, too, the ; most severe forms of bladder and urinary troubles Tincture Gentian Compound, one ounce; Syrup Rhubarb, one ounce ; Liquid Barkola Compoued, one I ounce ; and Syrup of Ginger, five ounce. Mix by shaking well in a bottle, and take one teaspoonful after each meal, and one before retiring for the night. The ingredients can be had from any good chemist, and being purely vegetable, are entirely harmless, are safe to use at any time.

THOLUCK'S CHRISTMAS-TREE.

The Christmas-tree is sometimes called Christ's tree, by the G4rmans. Their children usually speak of it as the sugar-tree; for, in their eyes, its attractiveness is due to the candies and sweetmeats which it brings. The tree has taken root in our land. But its growth will be promoted if we surround it with the simple tastes and quiet " amusableness " which have made it grow so luxuriantly in its native soil.

An English clergyman, while a student at the University of Halle, was invited to celebrate Christmas Eve at the house of that eminent Christian scholar, the late Professor Tholuck. His description exhibits the simplicity and child-likeness cf mind with which the Gerrnans enjoy their Christmas-tree. }-le says:

"Down each side of the room into which we entered—there were sixty students invited—was a long table. In the middle of each table was placed a large Christmas-tree profusely lighted up with little wax tapers and decorated with cGloured and gilt papers. "On each side of the two tables were ranged plates filled with small cakes and sweetmeats. Before each plate lay a kind of plum-loaf, and at the side a book or books.

"The room opened into the professor's study, in which there was also a table. On it were a Christmas tree, a miniature representation of the sliepheAls t2nding their flocks, of the ap- pearance of the angels, and of the manger, with it heavenly Child, with Mary and Joseph standing near. There also were plates with cakes, books, and small articles of dress. "As soon as the visitors had greeted their hcst and hostess, four students struck up a chorale.

"When they ceased, Professor Tholuek re- minded his guests of the great gift which the festival commemorated, and begged them to accept the little gifts he had laid by their plates..

" Each one was then requested to look for his plate and book, which was indicated by a slip bearing his name. On every slip Tholuck had also written an aphorism, such as: Thou, 0 God, hast made us for Thyself, and our heart is restless, until it rests in Thee ' ; 'We can only know God by living a divine life'; 'First receive the kingdom of God as a little child, then press into it as a man.' "lea wits then handed around, and the company separated into mall groups for conversation. After they had thus spent the hours, Tholuck read a few verses in the New Testament, commenting on them, and closed the evening with a brief prayer. "The guests then gathered their cakes, leaves, sweetmeats, and books, and bidding their hest and hostess good-night, departed, filled with a quiet delight."

POMPW AND VANITIES. • A rather pompous, gaily-dressed lady said to her Sunday class: "Give me an illustration of the pomps and vanities of the world."

The answer of one of her pupils was honest but unexpected: "Them flowers in your hat, miss." NOT FAIR.

Mollie had been to church for the first time, and on her return home her grandmother asked her what she thought of it. "I liked it very much," che replied, "bus there was one thing I didn't think was fair." " What was that, dear?" "Why, one man did all the work, and then another came round and got all the money."

A RIDDLE. Question: Why is it dangerous to go out in the wood in spring? • Answer: Because the bulrush is out; The oxslip about; The flowers have pistils; And the twigs are shooting.

SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS

THE CHELIFER, OR "FALSE SCORPIOIC." A note in the Zoologeftt reeorils the occur- rence in n; Manchrster 'bakehouse of a species of chelifor which: has only iltely become known .as a native cf Erito.in.: Facts rather point to an introduction from abroad. Fot up to 1892 only four specimens had been re- corded, while since that date it has turned up in -many parts of the country. The chelifers are the nearest approach to true scorpions -which occur in this country, and being very scorpi,on-like in shape, have .obt•sined the title of " false scorpion.' One Cr .1116T1 is the book-scorpion, an inhabiant of old, books, while others are parasites on flies. •

BACTERIAL LOCOMOTION. While some bacteria are quiet, others move about rapidly. The movements are produced, says Science Siftings, by the swinging of whip-like protoplasmic processes known ,as the flagella or cilia. The flagella may be borne singly or inl numbers at one end of the body, or may be i scattered over the entire surface. In most bacteria we can distinguish a permanent longit dinal axis, and along this . axis movement taken place. Thus both the : form, and, in coqespondence with it, the ' movement, are more definite than in anunba. . If the bacterium is quiet, we can predict that when it moves it will be in the direction of this axis. For ameba, such a prediction can- not be made. In some bacteria the two ends are similar and movement may take place. in either direction. In others the No ends differ, one bearing flagella while the other . does not. DEATH AND EVOLUTION. Death, remarks 'Dr. Minot, in a lecture, is not 'a universal accompaniment of life. In the lower organisms death does not occur as a natural and necessary result of life. Death with them is purely the result of an accident, some external cause. Natural death is a thing which has been acquired in the process of evolution. Why should it have been ac- quired? It is due to differentiation. When the cells acquire the additional faculty of passing beyond the simple stage to the more complicated organisation they lose some- thing of their vitality, something of their possibilities of perpetuation; and as the organisation in the process of evolution becomes higher and higher this necessity for chanr becomes more and more imperative. But it involves the end. Differentiation leads up, as its inevitable conclusion, to' death. It is the price which the higher organisms must pay for their power of differentiation.

===== 0002601_19080111_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
POISON IN PERFUMES.

It is believed that strong perfumes have power to intoxicate and benulb, and though workers in perfume laboratories are occasion- ally so much affected as to need medical aid, . _ _

little attention has been mid by physiologists to the effect of odours. But a German physi- cian has made a series of exneriments which fully confirm the popular belief. The experi- ments were not made on human beings, but on frogs, which were put under glass with sponges saturated with various essences. The effects are similar to those of chloroform. There is a brief stage of excitement, followed by partial or complete paralysis. Although many odours were used, they were found to differ in rapidity of action, and the notori- ously heavy and " beady " musk was found to be one of the slowest. Camphor, peppermint, lavender, and cloves are also slow, while aldehyde, turpentine, elder flower, ylang ylang, "peau d'Espagne," asafcetida, bisul- phuret of carbon, mustard, and nicotine act quickly and energetically.

===== 0002601_19080111_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
RATE OF FORMATION OF COAL.

At a meeting of the American Society of Mechanical Engineers, Mr. F. E. Junge set forth some interesting facts regarding the rate of formation and the rate of consump- tion of coal. The great German chemist, Liebig, calculated that a hectare (nearly 21 acres) of farm, meadow, or forest land in middle Europe would produce 21 tons of dry organic matter. This is chiefly cellulose, which contains ,40 per cent, of carbon, and it is estimated that at the above rate the total annual coal production of the globe would be

13,000 MILLION TONS. Only about 1 per cent, of this potential coral, however, is actually preserved as peat, lig- nite, coal, oil, or natural gas. The annual consumption of coal, on the other hand, is estimated at 1,000 million tons. Thus, al- though the potential yearly formation of coal is thirteen times the annual consumption, the actual storing up of fuel smounts to little over an eighth part of that consumed. And Mr. Junge points out that of the coal con- sumed hardly 5 per cent, of its calorific power is actually utilised as heat, light, or power.

===== 0002601_19080111_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
BIRDS AND THE DARK DAYS.

In an interesting address to the Scottish Natural History Society, Professor Schafer calls attention to the influence of davliaht in determining bird migration. Put briefly, the explanation of migration set forth by Pro- fessor Schafer ia, that the summer migrants come north to take advantage of the longer days, and go south again to avoid the short and dark ones. They require all their time to get sufficient food for thems:elves and their voracious young, and hence the extra daylight of the north in summer is an advantage to them. , In winter the short days do not allow them time to get sufficient. For most birds, it is pointed out, .require daylight for feeding. Professor Schafer points out various objec- tions to the old explanations that migration is due to

===== 0002601_19080111_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
COLD AND WANT OF FOOD.

Many species, for example, leave this country while it is still warm, and food still abund- ant. Nor does he admit that the migratory instinct may have originated in the cold of the glacial epoch driving the birds south- wards. The migratory instinct, he points out, is so widespread that it must have been evolved along with the powers of flig7it. And in Tertiary times a mild climate extended over North Polar regions, so that . the sug- gested cause was absent when the instinct was evolved.

It has been noticed by many observers that birq migration is largely independent of weather, and this is difficult to ex.plain on the assumption that temperature is one of the chief determining factors. But if, as Profes- sor Schafer thinks, the

RELATIVE LENGTH OF DAYLIGHT • is the cause, then the times of migration might be expected to be as regular as the lengthening of the days. The difficulty that the spring migration begins before the vernal equinox, when the days are still longer in the South, while the autumn migration begins while they are still longer in the North, is noticed, but, the 0/osc thinks, not very satisfactorily disposed of. Some time ago, it is pointed out, Seebohm, in his monograph on the plovers, suggested that they migrated, not in,search of food or warmth, but of light. And a Swedish poet, Enneberg, reflecting on the autumn departure of the birds from his country, concluded that it was a

===== 0002601_19080111_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
LONGING AFTER LIGHT

which led. them South. The motto of the bird of passage, he said, is Lux inea dux. The dif- ference between these views and thoe of Pro- fessor Schafer is, that the latter ' dreg not think that migrating birds are attracted by light as light, but only as a means of allowing them time to obtain a larger supply of food. Like other theories of migration, that of Professor Shafer is not without its diffienl- ties. Is there, it may be asked, sufficient dif- ference in the length of a day in this country and in North Africa to make it worth a swal- low's while ,to leave the sunny shores of the Nile on account of the extra dayligm,? And if a short extra time of daylight is enough to determine migration .how is it that all, or ,most, of the swallows visiting Britain do not po to the North of Scotland? There apuear to be as many breeding en the south coast of I:ngland as in the Orkney and Shetland islands. One of the most accomplished of. modern ornithologists, Herr Giitke, declared that migration is a "riddle which has hitherto defied every attempt at a solution."-

er, Saturday January 11, 1908.

Matter intended for publication must be written on one side only of the paper and sent in, if possible, not sater than Thursday. The Mayor and the Nursing Association Sir,—Referring to my letter which appeared in your issue of December 21st, I have much pleasure in announcing that I have received the following further i promises towards the £3OO required to relieve the War- rington Nursing Association from debt, viz. :—William Long,Esq., F. Monks,Esg., F. W.Monks, Esq., Charles E. Parker, Esq., and Colonel Buckton. As I now only require five more contributors of £2O each to entitle the Association to the £3OO I make a further earnest appeal to my friends to help me to raise the balance.— I am, Sir, your obedient servant, JAMES SMETHIJRST, Mayor. .Town Hall, Warrington, 10th January, 1008.

===== 0002601_19080111_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
Junior Liberals.

Sir,—May I trespass on your valuable space, to bring before the notice of your readers the general move- ment that is making progress among young Liberals all over the country. There have. I think, always been a number of the younger members of the Liberal party who have wished to at least try to understand the problems of their own day. Unfortunately there is not always a good oppor- tunity at hand for the study of political questions. The movement to which I refer above is to supply this opportunity. Briefly this movement is an attempt to organise scattered Associations formed in various parts of the country for the study of political questi.,ns and also to promote the formation of kindred Associations in localities where this has not already been done. My reason for bringing this before your notice at the present moment is that the New Year sees the amalgamation of the" League of Young Liberals " with the Young British Liberals Federa- tion " the Lancashire and Cheshire branch of which your readers will remember organised the mass meeting in the Parr Hall last year :hen the Right Hon. S. Buxton M.P., was the principal speaker. The combined forces are to be known in the future as "The National League of Young Liberals" with head- quarters at 5, Duke-street, W.C. The Officers at present are : President, Franklin Thomasson Esq., M.P. ; Chairman of Executive,C. F. G.Masterman Esq., M.P. ; Treasurer, A. Partington Esq., M.P. ; Secretary, J. Aubrey Rees Esq. There are already 100 branches 20. being in London and 80 in the Provinces with a total member- ship of 13,000. Locally, arrangements are well in hand for the forma- tion of a Lancashire and Cheshire Federation (consist- ingof 15 branches and 1,500 members), which is to organise branches in this district and to help existing branches to be mutually helpful ; while in Warrington' the Junior Liberal Association has become a branch of the League. The main points in the policy of the League are : To promote Education on Progressive Principles. To maintain Free Trade, support the movement for the taxation of Land Values, to advocate Progress in Municipal Affairs and to support the Liberal Party. If any of your readers wish for further information about the League, either Mr. F. Godwin Lloyd, of 12, Stockport-road, Ardwick, Manchester. or myself will be pleased to give whatever information we can. In Warrington itself we shall be very pleased to welcome anybody desirous of joining our local branch at the weekly meetings, particulars of which are duly advertised in your columns.—Yours Sic„ HERBERT W. GUEST.

4. Palmyra-square. Is Interest Robbery ? Sir,—l am sorry that Mr. Reeves' last reply does nothing to help us to anssser the above question. In the instances I gave I showed that the hirers of horse and cart and of machine after paying to the capitalist some part ot the produce by way of interest have left for themselves a net gain in labour products over unassisted labour. The capitalist sacrificed again that which would have been his if instead of hiring out his capital be employed it for his own use during the period it was on hire. And I asked the question if the user claimed not only the net extra he got in his labour product, but refused to pay anything far hire or interest and appropriated this in addition, as advocated by Socialists, would not he be the robber? Why cannot this question be answered ? It is perti- nent to the issue we ate discussing, and goes to its root. Perhaps Mr. Reeves has over-looked it and can find time to answer it next week. If Mr. Reeves pleads that he has not time then let some other Socialist take it up. I want Socialists to stand to their guns or withdraw the charge that" Interest is Robbery. What we have good reason to complain of in the action of the Socialists is the frequency with which they make random charges and when asked to make them good, run away. They preach a creed which they are afraid when challenged to put in practice, a creed for show and not for use (see John Workman's last). They confuse capital with land and monopoly ; rent,the element of risk of loss of principal, wages of superin- tendence and profit with interest. All these are mixed up its a hopleess jumble, and brought into the dock under the names of capital and interest and charged and condemned as robbers. Arising out of an instance I gave to show that capital assists labour and therefore ought to be rewarded by the payment of interest, Mr. Reeves misquotes my question as follows :—" Has not the inventor of the sewing machine to be paid for his labour ?" and replies "Certainly he should be, but the present system does not decide where the payment for inventors shall end. 4.tc..' Now I never said anything about the inventor of the machine, 1 never used the word. What I did speak of was the maker of the machine,and Mr. Reeves should not ascribe to me a word I never used. The payment of inventors is an entirely different matter, and has nothing to do with capital and interest, and 1 deprecate confusing the discussion by enlarging its limits. Let us get one subject settled at a time. Mr. Reeves says 1 have dropped the query "[s capital the enemy of labour ?" Not at all, that is the question I tried to answer by my illustration. I showed by a typical instance that capital was labour's friend and helpeethat the use of capital in the form of the sewing machine saved the seamstress 3s. after paying Is. interest for the use of the machine. Mr. Reeves asks me if I think a number of the people in Warrington are comfortably well off because 15,465 have on the average ,C 27 in the Savings Bank. I do not see that it matters a whit in regard to the definite issue between us what I think on this point, although it would have its importance if we were dis- cussing other issues. I mentioned the fact along with a reference to other channels in which the working people invest capital and earn interest, to show that the state- ment of Mr. Reeves that the present form of ownership of capital divides Society into two great classes was far from accurate, and to ascertain if possible where his supposed dividing line was drawn. The fact is there is no such division. There is no dividing line or chasm, but what is more akin to a very gradual upward slope which is continuous from bottom to top and which happily our people as a whole are steadily if slowly ascending. I regret that Mr. Reeves indicates that he has no time further to continue this correspondence. If be has less time than I have he is sadly over-worked and would have some right to claim to being " A White Slave." He is entirely mistaken if he thinks that I have leisure to engage in this and like correspondence as a sort of " intellectual gymnastics." To me the matter is too important, too serious, to regard from the light of an intellectual amusement. The general subject of which this issue is a part affects the well-being of every unit of the community ; it is one which profoundly concerns the well-being of the race. Upon it much of the future comfort and happiness of our people depends and therefore calls for the earnest thought, and sacrifices if need be, of all who care for the commonwealth and commonweal. To look upon the only outcome of this argument as resulting in the conclusion "We agree to differ " seems to me to call in question the sovereignty of truth and the power of human minds honestly to appreciate and acknowledge the right that is brought forward. Both of us cannot be right, and for my part if Mr. Reeves can show me where I am wrong be will he find me will- ing and ready to acknowledge it, on the other hand if he can be shown to be wrong, I doubt not, as an honest man, be will acknowledge it. To anticipate that such discussions will only culminate in an agreement to differ it is a counsel of despair. 1 I am obliged to Mr. Reeves for suggesting for perusal Ruskin's " Unto this Last" and Karl Marx's Capital." lam fairly familiar with both these works, especially with the latter,but a re-perusal will no doubt be useful and if I can cast my opinion of the teachings of Karl Marx, in his undoubtedly able work, into a form which will be la general interest I may send it for publication. CHARLES E. PARKER. Penketh, January 7th, 1908.

EXTRACTS FROM " PROGRF.S AND POVERTY." BY HENRY GEORGE. - CliAl"rEß 4.

"The belief that interest is the robbery of industry is, I am persuaded, in large part due to a failure ta discriminate between what is really capital and what is not, and between profits which are properly interest and profits which arise from other sources than the use of capital. In the speech and litera- ture of the day everyone is styled a capitalist who possesses what, independent of his labour, will yield him a return, while whatever is thus received is spoken of as the earnings or takings of capital, and we everywhere hear of the conflict of labour and capital. Attention has already been called to the fact that land values, which constitute such an enormous part of what is commonly called capital, are not capital at all ; and that rent, which is as commonly included in the receipts of capital, and which takes ' an ever increasing portion of the produce of an advancing community, is not the earnings of capital ' and must be carefully separated from interest. Nothing can be capital, let it always be remem-•

===== 0002601_19080111_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
The Examiner, Saturday, January 11, 1908.

When James I. granted to his minion the OLD ACADEMY. exclusive privilege of making gold and silver thread and prohibited, under severe penalties, every one else from making such thread, the income which Buckingham enjoyed in consequence did not arise Interesting Reminiscences. from the interest upon the capital invested in the manufacture, nor from the skill, &c., of those who ; really conducted the operations, but from what he Mr. FREDERICK MONKS, J.P.' who recently left War- got from the King—viz., the exclusive privilege— ringtou to live in retirement at Southport, was enter- in reality the power to levy a tax for his own pur- tamped by the Warrington Society at a complimentary poses upon all the users of such thread. NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS. RUGBY FOOTBALL By " Ovalist." Frost cancelled nearly all football on Saturday, and only four Northern League matches were played. Warrington should have met Salford, but the Wilders- pool ground was hard as adamant. No doubt the club will lose financially as a result of the postponement, but in other respects Warrington were fortunate in not having to play. Owing to injuiries they would have been obliged to put a weak team in the field and were by no means certain of victory. When the match does come off, the homesters may be better situated as regards players, and in that case there will be no fear of the result.

LETTERS OF APOLOGY.

Mr. E. J. HALL announced the receipt of numerous letters of apology for non-attendance. The following are extracts from some of them : _

Mr. WILLIAM LONG (president of the Society) : We must all feel grieved that Mr. Monks has found it neces- sary to sever his connection with the business life of his native town where he has so long borne so strenuous and honourable a part, but we will hope that the step he has taken will have the effect of prolonging a life that we feel law will still be devoted to the public good wherever he may be. He has set a noble example to the younger generations in Warrington and shown that it is possible to combine successfully attention to a large and growing manufacturing concern employing large numbers of men, with a keen and active personal interest in the municipal, social and other work of the town and neighbourhood. He has well earned the leisure he seeks and may he live long to enjoy it, occasionally looking up his old friends in Warrington .and especially at the Old Academy to which he has been a good friend,and he may rest assured we will take care of his " old Oliver " and try to keep his memory green. Rev. CANON STEVENSON: I should like to have had the pleasure of meeting Mr. Monks and at the same time testifying to the respect in which he is held both in regard of his personal character and of 311 the , generous aid and civic service which he has rendered to Warrington. Mr. J. MALIN EDELSTEN : I quite agree that Mr. 11VIonks's valuable services to the town of Warrington ' and his interest in the Warrington Society merit the deepest gratitude. • Letters were also received from Dr. Markel, Messrs. William Monks, J. W. Slack, W. H. J. Jenkins, J. A. Ransoms, Martin Nicholls, T. J. Lee and C. Jackson Holmes.

Though there has been no play there are plenty of interesting football topics. First and foremost comes the international match between New Zealand and England at Wigan to-day. This is probably the most important match of the All Blacks' tour, and they will be anxious to beat the chosen of England, especially after having failed by a single point to beat Wales.

This is the first time the Northern Union authorities havn had the pleasure of picking a genuine English international side, and everyone is interested in the kind of job they have made of it. The team was announced on Saturday and is,as follows :

Full-back : H. Taylor (Hull). Three-quarters: A. Hogg (Broughton Rangers), left centre ; J. L9mas (Stlford), right centre ; J. Leytham (Wigan) left wing; and P. Eccles (Halifax), right wing. Hall-backs: H. Wallace (Hull) and J. Flynn (Broughton Rangers). Forwards : Padbury (Runcorn), Wilson (Hunslet), Fergusson and Smith (Oldham), A. Robinson (Halifax) and Beetham (Broughton Rangers). Reserves : Full-back, W. Little (Halifax) ; centre three-quarter, G. Dickenson (Warrington) ; wing three-quarter, H. Farrar (Hunslet) ; half-back, J. Baxter (Rochdale Hornets) ; forwards, J. Bartle (Halifax), B. Avery (Oldham), and J. Clampitt (Broughton Rangers).

To say that the team gives general satisfaction would not be anywhere near the truth. No Inter- national side ever did give satisfaction before the match. There is always a host of critics ready to cavil at the selections and scoff at the team. Person- ally, I feel like joining the scoffers, for the first real English N.U. team is surprisingly constituted. Neither Oldham nor Hunslet, the only undefeated teams in the Union, have a single back selected. The full back and threequatters will pass muster, with the exception of Hogg. I always understood that the Broughtonian was a Scotchman. He certainly came from Hawick in Scotland, and unless he is of English birth he should never have been given the preference over Gecrge Dickenson, the first reserve. Exception might be taken to Lomas on this season's form, but he is capable of such brilliant work that in the absence of Wilson he is a safe selection.

The selection of the half backs is the most amazing, and it would be interesting to know the reasons which actuated the Selection Committee in choosing such an odd pair as Wallace (Hull) and Flynn (Broughton Rangers). Wallace has played for Yorkshire, but has by no means a great reputation outside his own district. Albert Goldthorpe, the skilful leader of the Hunslet team, ought to have been given the preference before any Lther half in the Union. Years notwithstanding he is still without a superior at the base of the scrum, and never had an equal as a scorer of goals. But what about Flynn, the man whom Swinton parted with and who has never had a regular place even with Broughton ? In fact it is not certain even that Flynn is a half back, and to cap him before such class men aslWhite (Oldham), Brooks (Warrington), Jolley (Runcorn), Hilton (Halifax), is an incomprehensible choice. Broughton Rangers have three men in the team and it looks as if they had been specially favoured in the distribution of inter- national honours. There is room for improvement in the forwards, but on the whole they are a good lot, The young Runcorn lad, Padbury, is to be congratu- lated on the honour which has so soon been given him.

Taken all round, the English team is not one which inspires great confidence, and- I should not be sur- prised to see the All Blacks win to-day. Combination among such a mixed lot can hardly be up to the best standard and if the halves do not come off there is sure to be some hesitancy behind. I should like to have seen Dickenson chosen. Be is well worth his place this season and may get bis chance yet seeing that he is first reserve for both England and Lancashire.

The Lancashire match with the All Blacks tikes place on January 18th, and the following is the selected thirteen from the County Palatine. Back: H. Gifford (Barrow). Three-quarters : J. Leytham (Wigan), left wing ; B. Jenkins (Wigan), left centre; A. Hogg (Broughton Rangers) (capt.), right centre ; G. Tyson (Oldham), right wing. Half-backs : J. Baxter (Rochdale), J. Jolley (Runcorn). Forwards : R. Padbury (Runcorn), A. Smith (Oldham), J. Beetham (Broughton Rangers), F. Ruddick (Broughton Rangers), F. H. Shugars (War- rington), and A. Buckler (Salford).

Reserves.—J. Sharrock ( Wigan), full-back ; G. Dickenson (Warrington), centre three-quarter ; C. Creevey (St. Helens), wing three-quarter ; W. D. John (Salford), half-back ; B. Avery (Oldham), J. Spencer (Salford) and W. Cheatham (Wigan), forwards.

This is, in my opinion a better team than the English side, and would be further strengthened by putting Dickenson right centre and Hogg wing, in place of Tyson. After all the warnings he has had Jack Fish has missed the highest honours of hi b career. Everybody had hoped that he would have made special efforts this season to keep fit and in form to take his place in the English team. But his selection was impossible in the face of ,his recent weak displays and obvious want of training. Fish would still be the best wing in England if he lived the life of an athlete. To-day even the Lancashire team can do without him. How long will it be before Warrington knows him no mole as a playing m,.mber of the club ?

Congratulations to Frank Shugars on his Lwcashi re cap. The burly Welshman is a rare worker in the scrum and deserves to follow in the steps of Board- man, Preston, Thomas and Naylor as County for- wards.

If frost does not cause another postponement to-day Warring-ton should journey to Barrow to play the return fixture with the Furness men. Lees, Jenkins and Taylor are still absent through injury and the selected team is :—Tilley, full back ; Fish, Isherwood, Diclieason and Jordan, three-quarters; Brooks and Lloyd, half-backs; Thomas, Shugars, A. Boardman, P. Boardman, Belton and Heath, forwards.

--- The " A team is due to play a Combination match at Wilderspool with Pemberton. Team :—T. Knight, full back ; Lander, Kenyon, Tinkler and Harrison, three-quarters ; Cartwright and Daintith, half-backs ; Jolley, Forster, Kettle, Morley, Holbrook and Preston, forwards.

-- When Warrington played Runcorn on New Year's Day one of the excited Runcorn pressmen sent a report to the " Daily Dispatch " which stated that " Harrison was deliberately kicked in the chest by a Warrington forward." I remember the incident, and while there was some rather reckless footwork there was certainly nothing like a deliberate kick at the Runcorn full back. The Warrington Club were annoyed at this reflection on their sportrnanship and Mr. Ashton wrote the " Despatch with the result that an explanation appeared on Wednesday, which accepted the Warrington version of the incident.

"OUR GUEST-MR. MONKS."

Mr. H. ROBERTS proposed the toast of the evening. He said he wished for many reasons that the toast had fallen into other hands. One reason was that he felt that as in some degree a contemporary of the guest of the evening he found himself getting to a period in years which unfortunately one did not reflect upon with a particular amount of gratification. On the other hand he would yield to no-one in the desire to pay in public that testimony of gratitude which every right-minded citizen in this town must feel towards one who for so many years had devoted the best of his intellect and his physical strength to the service of his fellow-men. (Applause.) They were honouring them- selves in taking that opportunity of not allowing their guest to depart from that intimate association which he had held so long with their town, without some recognition of a public character of the value of the work he had done. There were many sides to the character of their guest—his determination and pluck and his kindly generosity in regard to which he could say without any hesitation that there were many families in the town and district who were to-day grateful for the fact that Mr. Monks was a friend of theirs when his friendship was shown in a practical form and tended to alleviate burdens which wore heavy to bear. He (Mr. Roberts) did not know any man— and he spoke from a more or less intimate knowledge of public affairs in Warrington extending considerably over 30 years—who had in so varied a capacity served his town so well and in so distinguished a manner as their honoured guest. air. Monks had always had a high ideal as to the duties of public citizen- ship and, as far as a man could, he had lived well up to that ideal. As early as 1867 he entered' the rown Council of which distinguished body (laughter)—well, it was a distinguished body in those days, at any rate, and he dare say it was more dis- tinguished in some respects to-day than it was then— (laughter)—he was a member for 17 years. At that time it was necessary for a man entering the Council to possess a qualification proving him to be worth at least £l,OOO over and above his liabilities, but with the growth of democracy, of which some of them were proud, that qualification had been entirely dispensed with, and now it NV,S not necessary for a man to be possessed of £l,OOO or 1,000 pence, but he ought to be' possessed, although he was not legally required to be, of common sense and honesty of purpose. Having eulogised Mr. Monks's work for the Museum and for Education, Mr. Roberts said the one thing Warrington had to regret in connection with Mr. Monks was the fact that the town never honoured itself by making him its chief magistrate. (Applause.) It did, however, to some extent compensate him and itself by making him a freeman of the borough, which was an even higher honour. Mr. Roberts referred to Mr. Monks's work as a County Councillor for ten years. as a Justice of the Peace for Warrington since 1880,and as a member of the County Bench for many years. Warrington owed to him one of the few statues which existed in.the country to one of England's greatest commoners, Oliver Cromwell. He need not refer to Mr.Monks's other gift to the town, because he (Mr. Roberts) was one of those obstinate and somewhat short-sighted men who opposed the taking down of the wall in front of the Town Hall, and he lived to admit that that was one of the few mistakes —(laughter)—that he made during the eighteen years be had the honour of serving the town as a member of the Corporation. Tangible and appreciable as the other mementoes of Mr. Monks's association amongst them were, there was another which was greater and would have more permanent benefit for the town and district. He referred to Mr. Monks's practical and benevolent foresight in instituting the Nurses' Home—(applause)— an institution which maintained five paid nurses and la superintendent, and which last year was the means of alleviating sickness and pain in 15,000 visits spread over 500 patients, an institution which was every hour of the day and every day of the week doing something to allay sorrow and suffering. That, he claimed, was the greatest of all the benefits Mr. Monks had conferred upon the town. Mr. Roberts concluded by applying to Mr. Monks the words of Whittier Such is our friend formed on the good old plan, A true and brave and downright honest man Who blows no trumpet in the market place, Nor in the church with hypocritic face Supplies with cant the lack of Christian grace; Loathing pretence, he does with cheerful will What others talk of, while their hands are still. (Applause.) The toast was honoured with great enthusiasm, the company singing "For he's a jolly good fellow." MR. MONKS'S REMINISCENCES, Mr. Motexs, in responding, said he felt it a very great honour to have that compliment paid to him. He had passed his seventy-third birthday and he thought that if ever a man was entitled to some rest from a strenu- ous life, an age like that entitled him to it. (Hear, hear.) If it would be any encouragement to younger men in business, he might say that the motto which he adopted was "Courage and Eolith." He was very early in life associated with two of the best men %Varringtots ever had—Philip Carpenter and William Robson and from them he imbibed the feeling that the highest life that a man could live for his fellow men was to devote himself to their service. He was profoundly thankful if the expressions which had fallen from Mr. Roberts represented the feeling of his townsmen, that he had been able to do it with clean hands. (Applause). Whatever bonoura had come to him had come unsought. He had never in the whole course of his life been a party to any intrigue and, like a man putting his armour off, he could honestly say that so far as he knew, he had never defrauded any man to the extent of a halfpenny. One of his first ideals was to work out if possible the principle of co-operative manufacturing. He started, in conjunc- tion with is friend whom he was glad to know was still living, the Warrington Co-operative Society with a view to the amelioration of the conditions of the working people and he subsequently tried to realise a manufacturing industry for the workmen themselves. But the jealousy, misunderstandings and misrepresenta- tions that one was subjected to completely demolished that ideal. As a man whom some of them would know, Henry Whitham—now living out in America—said, he had too much sense to go no beating the windmill sails and so that led to his forming the Whitecross Company. It was true that he had devoted a good many years of active service to the interests of the town in many ways —(bear, hear),—both religiously and politically. If he were to narrate some of the incidents of his political career they would probably be amused. One story he would relate, because it would show the innate goodness of a man who=e name he hoped would always ho remembered in Warrington— Gaorge Crosflehl. Mr. Monks referred to his own opposition to the proposal aNu;sition of one of the Arpley fields offered by Mr. Wilson-Patten aid said that after that scheme had been defeated he was talking Ito Mr. Crostleld who congratulated him on the stand he

Wycliffe Church.

New Year's &LC-lay was flttiogly observed at this church. In the morning there was a special service for young people conducted by the Rev. Alfred Lee,minister of Emmanuel Church, Bootle, who is always a welcome visitor to Wycliffe. He held the interest of the children by a capital address full of apt anecdotes 2nd illustrations. Special hymns were heartily sung. At the close of the service the Pastor (Rev. J. Yonge) dis- tributed Bibles to the 31 young people, about the age of 15, who had been attending bN annual Catachumen class. It was the 27th anniversary of that class, which has bad in the long course of years 770 young people in ite membership. Mr. Yonge said that many former members of the class had no doubt passed away, whilst others were scattered in many parts of the globe, and others were in the membership of Wycliffe and other churches in the town. ,From tokens and evidence which he vtas happy to receive from time to time he. knew the class had done great good, possibly to a great many more young people than he knew about. He hoped some of the lesons which the members had gained would linger in their memories and that the Bibles would find honoured places among their personal Possessions. In the evening the Pastor preached upon the Wycliffe motto for the New Year, Love the brotherhood "(E. Peter ii. 17). There was a large con- gregation. The Ordinance of the Lord's Supper was observed at the close of the service, when welcome was given in the name of the church to several new members.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church. On Sunday evening the pulpit at this church was occupied by the Rev. Sidney Pitt who took for his text" By love serve one another," Gal. 5, 13, which he suggested as a motto for the Church for the new year. In examining a large number of motto cards containing passages of scripture he had found very few texts which had anything to do with doing anything for anybody. Practical aspects of Christianity seemed to be largejy ignored by those who selected these contrivances. These aspects, however, must not be discarded. The most telling evidences of Christianity were the practical results produced in the world by Christian love and there were some people who would listen to no other. The Christian's duty was to be like his Lord and Mister who came not to be ministered unto unto but to minister and who gave 1-lis life a ransom for many. Christ washed the disciples' feat, the modern equivalent of which the preacher declared would be to black a person's boots. IN‘o service was too menial or too humiliating Mr Him to! undertake. Our service, however,must be in love. The 1 great defect of the Poor Law was that there had been for the last three hundred yeirs sadly too little of love in its administration and this was the peculiar value of those guilds of help and public health unions which were now being formed in many towns and for which there was great need in ‘Varrington. These institu- tions introduced ark element of Christian love and voluntary service in connection with INlunici pal adminis- ration which was of priceless value and already pro- duced most striking results.

PENKETH.

PENKETII, SANKEY & DISTRICT MD FOLK'S TREAT* —Last Friday evening the annual treat to the aged people took place in the Wesleyan schoolroom. Upwards of 90 sat down to a substantial tea. The tables were nicely decorated with plants, and the old folks thoroughly enjoyed the good things provided. At eight o'clock an excellent entertainment took place, when the Leyland Pierrots, from the well-known Leyland Shipping Company, of Liverpool, greatly amused the audience, every item teing applauded. Mrs. Fieldbloor, of Penketh, who is in her 73rd year, gave a fine exhibition of dancing in costume, of the " Highland Fling " and the" Sword Dance," accom- panied by Mrs. Owen on the piano. At the close supper was provided for the old folks. Tobacco, cigarettes, and pipes were supplied to the men, and the aged women were the recipients of a parcel of tea, coffee and sugar on leaving for home. Apples and oranges were also distributed.

TEA AND SOCIAE.—The Penketh Church Mission Women's Guild held their annual tea and social last Friday evening in the Infants' schoolroom. Tea com- menced at 6.30, when 66 sat down to a splendid tea. The tables had been beautifully decorated with plants. At 7.30 an excellent entertainment took place. The Rev. J. R. Jones, M.A., presided, the following artistes taking part :—Miss Jones, Mrs. Hannan, Mr. W. Ireland, Mr. Nocien, Mr. Wallace Ray, Mr. W. Moores, and Messrs. Gordon, Maurice and Burgess, who gave a short sketch" The Hard-up Rivals."

===== 0002601_19080111_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
STOCKTON HEATH.

Sr. THOMAS'S BAND OP HOPE.—A very pleasant meeting of the above took place in the schoolroom on Wednesday evening and this being the first after the holidays took the form of a children's party. Various games were entered into by members and workers alike and of the latter no one joined more heartily in the games than the Vicar, much to the happiness of the little folk. The entrance of a real Father Christmas in the person of Mr. W. Whiteman, carrying a large basket of toys, followed by Miss Crane, Mrs. Whiteman and Miss, Hallows, each with baskets of apples and oranges, soon put an end to the children's romps, and gathering round Father Christmas and his attendants they gave thew a real hearty reception. When quietness was restored and each and every child had received a share of the geed things, Mr. R. Whiteman very kindly gave several selections on his gramaphone which were enjoyed by all present. —At the close the Superintendent (Mr. Hallows) thanked all who had helped to make the evening sc. enjoyable, and especially the workers for the assistance given him in carrying the meetings so successfully thus far, and asked for a continuance from members and workers alike for the remainder of the session so that the concert by which they hoped to raise funds for the Summer Treat might be a grand success.

RAILWAY FATALITY AT WIDNES. ! .At Widnes, on Saturday, an inquiry was held into the death of Samuel Enight,aged sixty-four,residing with his cousin in Fensley Cross-lane,St.Helens.lt appeared that on the lst inst.,l: night said to his cnusin,Mrs.Lacey,that I he would take a walk to Widnes and see some friends. I At 1.45 in the afternoon he bought a railway ticket from I Appleton to Widnes. As the train did not go until 5.39 Ihe said tie would go and have a drirk. By some means or other he strayed away, and got on to the siding behind the Vine railway box. Daring shunting opera- tions at 5.30 he was knocked down by some waggons and badly mangled, his head being almost severed from his body. The jury,after hearing the evidenge,rettumed a verdict of" Aciclental death." NIAGARA A MERE CASCADE. A question which travellers often ask each other in varions parts of ths world (says the "Travel Magazine," New York), is : " What is really the greatest natural wonder on earth ?" It is easy an answer now, since the stupendous Falls of the Zambesi River have been dis- covered • ,ane of the world's mightiest rivers, two miles wide, falling sheer 420 foot. Niagara is only half a mile wide and Triß fret high, so that, it figures as a mere cascade in comparison.

RUGBY FOOTBALL. NORTHERN UNION. RESULTS OP SATURDAY'S MATCHES. G. T. P. G. T. P. New Zealand THE NORTHERN LEAGUE. Bradford *Oldham *Wigan *Rochdale H. 1 2 8 Runcorn 0 2 6 The following matches were postponed on account of froet :—Batley v. Wakefield Trinity, Dewsbury v. Hull, L'ramley v. Hunslet, Huddersfield v. York, Ebbw Vale v. Keighley, Widnes v. Barrow, Swinton v. Broughton Rangers, Warrington v. Salford, and Leigh v. Merthyr. ROCHDALE HORNETS v. RUNCORN. At Rochdale. About 4,000 spectators witnessed this match. The keen frost had made the ground very bard, but although Runcorn protested against the match being played, the referee decided that the ground was , fit. The visitors adopted the aggressive tactics right I away, and the game was not many minutes old when Jolley kicked over Rigg's bead, and Butterworth, beating Hartley in the race for the ball, scored. Jolley failed at goal. The Hornets then attacked, and a smart round of passing between Baxter, Schofield, micl Threlfall enabled the latter to score a try, which Farrar failed to convert. Threlfall, Ba7rter, and Farrar were prominent with dashing runs, and shortly afterwards the forwards rushed a scrummage near the Runcorn line, and Dearden scored. Baxter kicked a goal. Then the visitors raced away, and Jolley made a big effort to break through the defence, but he was tackled. Half-time score : Rochdale Hornets 1 goal, 2 tries (8 points), Runcorn 1 try (3 points). The game was resumed without interval. From a cross kick by Baxter, aibson had the misfortune to lose the ball, and a minute later he struggled over the line, but was ruled back. The visitors had another spell of attacking as the result of Schofield's kick being charged down. Harrison, the visiting full back, got possession from a long kick, and after a splendid run crossed at the cornet. Jolley failed to convert. Runcorn were penalised, and Baxter made a good,though unsuccessful, shot at goal. Result :—Rochdale Hornets 1 goal, 2 tries (8 points), Runcorn, 2 tries (6 points). WIGAN v. ST. HELENS. Although the ground at Central Park had been covered, the frost had hardened the playing pitch to such an extent that it was only a few minutes from the advertised hour of commencement when it was decided to open the gates and proceed with the game. Some 6,000 persons assembled to witness the hostilities. Four minutes after the kick off the " Saints " took the lead, Prescott rushing over from a scramble, and M. Creevey adding the goal. Wigan, however, soon drew level from a try secured by Treharne, Leytham having a successful shot at the St.Helens goal. Then Leytham can in a fine try, and added the second goal, whilst he got over again later on, his example being followed by Blears and Coulthard. Wigan started the second half with a margin of 14 points in their favour, and directly after the restart the Wigan captain registered his third try. Miller was the next to score, 'l'. Thomas gosling, and in the sub- sequent operations Cheetham and Silcock credited themselves with tries. Leytham landing his third goal. Early on the visitors lost the services of Lee, who had to be carried off the ground, whilst soon after the , interval the Wigan A'elshman, Treharne, had a fall and dislocated his shoulder. Result :—Wigan 4 goals 9 tries (35 points), St. Helens 1 goal 1 try (5 points).
